\mathbb{N}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\penalty\ \Phi(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},W_{s})\right)=E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}\bigg(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\Phi\big(J_{\tau_{D}},\widecheck{J}_{\tau_{D}},(\xi_{t}:t\leq\tau_{D})\big)\bigg),

(3.4)

see
[
11
, Proposition 4.3.2]
for a proof of this identity.
In particular, remark that we have

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\subseteq\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ \tau_{D}(W_{s})=H_{s}\},\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{--a.e.}

We stress that
L^{D}
is constant at every interval at which
W_{s}
stays in
D
and in each connected component of

\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ \tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}\}.

We call such a connected component an excursion interval from
D
. This family of intervals will be studied in detail in the next section. The process
L^{D}
is not measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{D}
, the informal reason being that it contains the information on the lengths of the excursions from
D
. However, as we are going to show in Proposition
3
, the time-changed process

\widetilde{L}^{D}:=\big(L_{\Gamma^{D}_{s}}^{D}\big)_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}

is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable – notice that we removed precisely from
L^{D}
by means of the time change the constancy intervals generated by excursions from
D
. This measurability property will be crucial for the proof of the special Markov property and the rest of this section is devoted to its proof.

First remark that we have only defined the exit local time under the measures
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x}
for
x\in D
. In order to be able to apply the Markov property, we need to extend the definition to more general initial conditions
(\mu,{\text{w}})\in\Theta
. This construction will also be essential for the results of Section
4
. The precise statement is given in the following proposition:

Proposition 2
.

Fix
(\mu,\rm{w})\in\Theta
such that
\rm{w}(0)

\in D
and suppose that
\mu(\{\tau_{D}(\mathrm{w})\})=0
. Then, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\rm{w}}
there exists a continuous, non-decreasing process
L^{D}
with associated Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}L^{D}
supported on
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}\in\partial D\}
, such that, for every
t\geq 0

L^{D}_{t}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}},

(3.5)

where the convergence holds uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\rm{w}})
. Moreover:

(i)

Under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\emph{w}}
, if
\tau_{D}(\emph{w})<\infty
, we have
L^{D}_{t}=0
for every
t\leq\inf\{s\geq 0:H_{s}<\tau_{D}(\emph{w})\}
.

(ii)

Under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\emph{w}}
, recall the definition of the random point measure
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},W^{i})}
defined in (
2.18
). Then we have:

L^{D}_{\infty}(\rho,W)=\sum\limits_{h_{i}<\tau_{D}(\emph{w})}L^{D}_{\infty}(\rho^{i},W^{i}),\quad\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\emph{w}}\text{--a.s.}

(3.6)

Proof.

Let us start with preliminary remarks and introducing some needed notation. Fix
(\mu,{\text{w}})\in\Theta
with
{\text{w}}(0)\in D
satisfying
\mu(\{\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\})=0
. We write

T_{r}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ H_{t}=r\},\text{ for every }r\geq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ T_{0}^{+}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\}.

By (
3.3
) and the strong Markov property, we already know that
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{T_{0}^{+}}^{T_{0}^{+}+t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}
converges as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\text{w}})
towards a non-decreasing continuous process supported on
\{t\geq T_{0}^{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}\in\partial D\}
. Consequently, it suffices to prove the proposition under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}
. In this direction, we set

\displaystyle I(t,\varepsilon):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}},

for every
\varepsilon>0
. Recall now that under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}
, the process
\langle\rho,1\rangle
is a killed Lévy process started at
\langle\mu,1\rangle
and stopped at its first hitting time of
0
. Write
((\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):\penalty\ i\in\mathbb{N})
, for the excursion intervals of
\langle\rho,1\rangle
over its running infimum, and let
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
be the subtrajectory associated with the excursion interval
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
. To simplify notation, we also set
h_{i}:=H(\alpha_{i})
and recall from (
2.18
) that the measure
\mathcal{M}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},W^{i})}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}W)
.

We suppose first that
\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\geq\zeta_{\text{w}}
. We shall prove that the collection
\big(I(t,\varepsilon),t\geq 0\big)
for
\varepsilon>0
is Cauchy in
L_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,{\text{w}}})
uniformly in compact intervals as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, viz.

\lim_{\delta,\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}\big[\sup_{s\leq t}|I(s,\varepsilon)-I(s,\delta)|\big]=0.

(3.7)

This implies directly the existence of
L^{D}
defined as in (
3.5
) as well as point (i). We shall then deduce (ii), and the remaining case
\tau_{D}({\text{w}})<\zeta_{\text{w}}
is treated afterwards. Let us proceed with the proof of (
3.7
). Since the Lebesgue measure of
\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=\inf_{s\leq t}\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\}
is null, we can write

\displaystyle I(t,\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\alpha_{i}\wedge t}^{\beta_{i}\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}},

which yields the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}\big[\sup_{s\leq t}|I(s,\varepsilon)-I(s,\delta)|\big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{s\leq t}\big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\alpha_{i}\wedge s}^{\beta_{i}\wedge s}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{D}(W_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\alpha_{i}\wedge s}^{\beta_{i}\wedge s}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{D}(W_{u})+\delta\}}\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{s\leq\sigma(W^{i})}\big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W^{i}_{u})<H(\rho_{u}^{i})<\tau_{D}(W^{i}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{s\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W^{i}_{u})<H(\rho_{u}^{i})<\tau_{D}(W^{i}_{u})+\delta\}}\big|\Big].

Since
\mu(\{\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\})=0
, the last display is given by

\int_{[0,\tau_{D}({\text{w}}))}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}\Big(\sup_{s\leq t}|I(s,\varepsilon)-I(s,\delta)|\Big).

(3.8)

Let us now show that (
3.8
) converges towards
0
when
\varepsilon,\delta\downarrow 0
. Since for every
h\in[0,\tau_{D}({\text{w}}))
we have
{\text{w}}(h)\in D
, the term inside the integral in (
3.8
) converges towards
0
as
\varepsilon,\delta\downarrow 0
by the approximation of exit local times under the excursion measure given in (
3.3
). Knowing that
\mu
is a finite measure, it suffices to show that the term,

\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}\Big(\sup_{s\leq t}|I(s,\varepsilon)-I(s,\delta)|\Big),

can be bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon,\delta
. However, still under
\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}
, we have the simple upper bound:

\sup_{s\leq t}|I(s,\varepsilon)-I(s,\delta)|\leq I(\sigma,\varepsilon)+I(\sigma,\delta),

and by the many-to-one formula (
2.20
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}\big(I(\sigma,\varepsilon)\big)=\varepsilon^{-1}E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{{\text{w}}(h)}\Big[\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\,\exp(-\alpha a)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(\xi)<H(J_{a})<\tau_{D}(\xi)+\varepsilon\}}\Big]\leq 1,

for every
\varepsilon>0
, where to obtain the previous inequality we use that
H(J_{a})=a
.
In particular, we have
\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}\big(I(\sigma,\varepsilon)+I(\sigma,\delta)\big)\leq 2
and (
3.7
) follows. Still under our assumption
\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\geq\zeta_{\text{w}}
we now turn our attention to (
3.6
). We know that for any
(h_{i},W^{i},\rho^{i})\in\mathcal{M}
we have the limit in probability:

L^{D}_{\sigma_{i}}(\rho^{i},W^{i})=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}.

It then follows from our definitions that for every
r>0
,

L_{\sigma}^{D}-L^{D}_{T_{\zeta_{{\text{w}}}-r}}=\sum_{h_{i}\leq\zeta_{\text{w}}-r}L^{D}_{\sigma_{i}}(\rho^{i},W^{i}),

observing that the number of non-zero terms on the right-hand side is finite.
By taking the limit as
r\downarrow 0,
we deduce (
3.6
) by monotonicity.

Let us now assume that
\tau_{D}({\text{w}})<\zeta_{\text{w}}
. To simplify notation, set
a:=\tau_{D}({\text{w}})
and notice that

(\rho_{T_{a}},W_{T_{a}})=\big(\mu\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\tau_{D}({\text{w}})]},({\text{w}}(h):h\in[0,\tau_{D}({\text{w}})])\big),

where we recall that
\mu(\{\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\})=0
. By our previous discussion and the strong Markov property, we deduce that
(I(t,\varepsilon)-I(T_{a},\varepsilon):t\geq T_{a})
converges as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\text{w}}})
towards a continuous process. To conclude our proof, it suffices to show that:

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\int_{0}^{T_{a}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\Big]=0.

To obtain the previous display, write

\int_{0}^{T_{a}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}=\sum\limits_{h_{i}\geq a}\int_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}},

where we have
h_{i}\neq a
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, since
\mu(\{a\})=0
. Moreover, for every
i
with
h_{i}>a
notice that
\tau_{D}(W_{s})=a
. This implies:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T_{a}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\leq\sum\limits_{a\leq h_{i}\leq a+\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma(W^{i})}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\varepsilon\}},

and we can now use that
\mathcal{M}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{{\text{w}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}W)
to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}\big[\int_{0}^{T_{a}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\big]\leq\mu([a,a+\varepsilon])N(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{0\leq H(\rho_{s})<\varepsilon\}}).

(3.9)

Finally, by the many-to-one formula (
2.20
), the previous display is equal to

\varepsilon\cdot\mu([a,a+\varepsilon])
, giving:

\limsup\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}\Big[\int_{0}^{T_{a}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{D}(W_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\Big]=\mu(\{a\})=0,

where in the last equality we use that
\mu\in\Theta
which ensures that
\mu(\{a\})=0
.
∎

Now that we have defined the exit local time under more general initial conditions, let us turn our attention to the measurabliliy properties of
\widetilde{L}^{D}
. From now on, when working under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
or
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, the sigma field
\mathcal{F}^{D}
should be completed with the
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
-negligible and
\mathbb{N}_{x}
-negligible sets respectively – for simplicity we use the same notation.

Proposition 3
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, the process
\widetilde{L}^{D}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable.

In particular, the proposition implies that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, the total mass
L^{D}_{\sigma}=\widetilde{L}^{D}_{\infty}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable. The proof will mainly rely on the two following technical lemmas.

Lemma 3
.

Consider an open subset
D\subset E
containing
x
. Fix an arbitrary
(\mu,\emph{w})\in\Theta
with
\emph{w}(0)=x
and satisfying
\mu(\{\tau_{D}(\emph{w})\})=0
if
\tau_{D}(\emph{w})<\infty
. Then, for every
K>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\emph{w}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\Big]=\int_{0}^{\mu\left([0,\tau_{D}(\emph{w}))\right)}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{\emph{w}(H(\kappa_{\langle\mu,1\rangle-u}\mu))}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle\leq K-u\}}\right).

Proof.

Recall that, under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}
, the process
\langle\rho,1\rangle
is a Lévy process started at
\langle\mu,1\rangle
and stopped at its first hitting time of
0
. As usual, write
\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):\,i\in\mathbb{N}\}
for the excursion intervals of
\langle\rho,1\rangle-\langle\mu,1\rangle
over its running infimum, that we still denote by
I
. We write
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
for the subtrajectory associated with
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
. As explained in (
2.14
), the measure:

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(-I_{\alpha_{i}},\rho^{i},W^{i})},

is a Poisson point measure with intensity

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\langle\mu,1\rangle]}(u){\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbb{N}_{\text{w}(H(\kappa_{u}\mu))}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}W).
Furthermore, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
H(\kappa_{-I_{\alpha_{i}}}\mu)=H_{\alpha_{i}}=H_{\beta_{i}}
and to simplify notation we denote this quantity by
h_{i}
. Next, we notice that, by Proposition
2
, we have
\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle-\langle\mu,1\rangle=I_{s}\}}=0
and
L^{D}_{t}=0
, for every
t\leq\inf\{s\geq 0:H_{s}<\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\}
. From our previous observations, we get:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}=\sum\limits_{h_{i}<\tau_{D}({\text{w}})}\int_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}=\sum\limits_{H(\kappa_{-I_{\alpha_{i}}}\mu)<\tau_{D}({\text{w}})}\int_{0}^{\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}(\rho^{i},W^{i})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s}^{i},1\rangle\leq K-\langle\mu,1\rangle-I_{\alpha_{i}}\}},

where we used in the second identity that
\langle\rho_{s+\alpha_{i}},1\rangle=\langle\rho_{s}^{i},1\rangle+\langle\rho_{\alpha_{i}},1\rangle=\langle\rho_{s}^{i},1\rangle+I_{\alpha_{i}}+\langle\mu,1\rangle
, for every
s\in[0,\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}]
. This implies that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\text{w}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\sum\limits_{H(\kappa_{-I_{\alpha_{i}}}\mu)<\tau_{D}({\text{w}})}\int_{0}^{\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}(\rho^{i},W^{i})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s}^{i},1\rangle\leq K-\langle\mu,1\rangle-I_{\alpha_{i}}\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt=\int_{\mu([\tau_{D}({\text{w}}),\infty))}^{\langle\mu,1\rangle}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\mathbb{N}_{\text{w}(H(\kappa_{u}\mu))}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K-\langle\mu,1\rangle+u\}}\Big),

and the desired result now follows by performing the change of variable
u\text{ \reflectbox{$\longmapsto$}}\,\langle\mu,1\rangle-u
and applying the many-to-one formula (
3.4
).
∎

Lemma 4
.

Consider an increasing sequence of open subsets
(D_{n}:\penalty\ n\geq 1)
containing
x
, such that
\cup_{n}D_{n}=D
and
\overline{D_{n}}\subset D
. There exists a subsequence
(n_{k}:\penalty\ k\geq 0)
converging towards infinity, such that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,\sigma]}|L_{s}^{D_{n_{k}}}-L_{s}^{D}|=0,\>\>\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{--a.e. }

(3.10)

Proof.

The proof of this lemma will be achieved by using similar techniques as in
[
19
, Proposition 2.3]
in the Brownian setting. We start by showing that, for a suitable subsequence, the total mass
L_{\sigma}^{D_{n}}
converges towards
L_{\sigma}^{D}
,
\mathbb{N}_{x}
-a.e. The uniform convergence will then be deduced by standard techniques. Notice however that in
[
19
]
, this is mainly done by establishing an
L_{2}(\mathbb{N}_{x})
convergence of
L^{D_{n}}_{\sigma}
towards
L^{D}_{\sigma}
, and that we do not have a priori moments of order
2
in our setting. In order to overcome this difficulty, we need to localize the tree by the use of a truncation argument. We start by showing that, for any fixed
K>0
, we have:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}=\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}},\quad\text{ in }L_{2}(\mathbb{N}_{x}).

(3.11)

In this direction, we write
\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big|\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big|^{2}\Big)
in the following form

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle-2\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\cdot\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\Big),

(3.12)

and the proof of (
3.11
) will follow by computing each term separately and by taking the limit as
n\uparrow\infty
. First, we remark that

\displaystyle\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{u}^{D}\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{u},1\rangle\leq K\}},

and the idea now is to apply the Markov property. For convenience, we let
\Theta_{D}
be the subset of
\Theta
of all the pairs
(\mu,{\text{w}})
satisfying the condition
\mu(\{\tau_{D}({\text{w}})\})=0
when
\tau_{D}({\text{w}})<\infty
, and we define
\Theta_{D_{n}}
similarly replacing
D
by
D_{n}
. Notice that by Lemma
2
, we have,
\mathbb{N}_{x}
–a.e.,
(\rho_{t},W_{t})\in\Theta_{D}\cap(\cap_{n\geq 1}\Theta_{D_{n}})
for every
t\geq 0
. For
(\mu,{\text{w}})\in\Theta_{D}
, we set

\displaystyle\phi_{D}(\mu,\text{w})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\text{w}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu\left([0,\tau_{D}({\text{w}}))\right)}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{{\text{w}}(H(\kappa_{\langle\mu,1\rangle-u}\mu))}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle\leq K-u\}}\right),

where in the second equality we used Lemma
3
. Note that the dependence of
\phi_{D}
on
K
is being omitted to simplify the notation. By our previous discussion, an application of the Markov property gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{N}_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{D}(\rho_{s},W_{s})\right)

\displaystyle=2E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{{D}}(J_{\tau_{D}},\xi^{\tau_{D}})\right),

(3.13)

where to simplify notation, we write

\xi^{\tau_{D}}:=(\xi_{t}:0\leq t\leq\tau_{D})
. Observe that
(J_{\tau_{D}},\xi^{\tau_{D}})\in\Theta_{D}
since by independence, we have
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}J_{\tau_{D}}(\{\tau_{D}\})=0
,
P^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}
–a.s.
Replacing
D
by
D_{n}
, we also have
(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})\in\Theta_{D_{n}}
and we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big)=2E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D_{n}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{{D_{n}}}(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})\right),

(3.14)

where for
(\mu,{\text{w}})\in\Theta_{D_{n}}
, we write

\phi_{D_{n}}(\mu,\text{w})=\int_{0}^{\mu\left([0,\tau_{D_{n}}({\text{w}}))\right)}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{{\text{w}}(H(\kappa_{\langle\mu,1\rangle-u}\mu))}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D_{n}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle\leq K-u\}}\right).

Our goal now is to take the limit in (
3.14
) as
n\uparrow\infty
and to show that this limit is precisely (
3.1
). In this direction, we remark that under
\{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle\leq K\}
, we have the trivial bound
\phi_{D_{n}}(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})\leq K
. Thanks to the dominated convergence theorem, it is then enough to show that,
P^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}
-a.s., the following convergence holds:

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D_{n}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{{D_{n}}}(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{{D}}(J_{\tau_{D}},\xi^{\tau_{D}}).

In order to prove it, we start noticing that we always have
\tau_{D_{n}}\uparrow\tau_{D}
as
n\to\infty
.
In particular, since
\langle J_{\infty},1\rangle=\infty
, we see that the limit in the previous display is
0
under
\{\tau_{D}=\infty\}
. Let us focus now on the event
\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}
. First remark that

\kappa_{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle-u}J_{\tau_{D_{n}}}=\kappa_{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle-u}J_{\tau_{D}},

for every
u\leq\langle J\tau_{D_{n}},1\rangle
. This combined with the independence between
J
and
\xi
ensures that, under
\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}
, the quantities
\langle J\tau_{D_{n}},1\rangle
and
\phi_{D_{n}}(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})
convergence
respectively to
\langle J\tau_{D},1\rangle
and
\phi_{D}(J_{\tau_{D}},\xi^{\tau_{D}})
, giving the desired convergence under
\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}
. Consequently, we get:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big).

Turning our attention to the cross-term, we can apply similar steps and the Markov property as before to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\cdot\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{u}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{u},1\rangle\leq K\}}\Big)+\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{u}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{u},1\rangle\leq K\}}\Big)

\displaystyle=E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D_{n}})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{D}(J_{\tau_{D_{n}}},\xi^{\tau_{D_{n}}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle J_{\tau_{D}},1\rangle\leq K\}}\phi_{D_{n}}(J_{\tau_{D}},\xi^{\tau_{D}})\right),

and using the same method as before we get:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\cdot\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big)^{2}\Big).

Taking the limit as
n\uparrow\infty
in (
3.1
) we deduce the claimed
L_{2}(\mathbb{N}_{x})
convergence (
3.11
). Now that the convergence of the truncated total mass has been established, to derive the statement of the proposition we proceed as follows. First, we introduce the processes

A^{n}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\>\>\>\text{ and }\>\>\>A_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}},

which are continuous additive functionals of the Markov process
(\rho,W)
. Then using the Markov property, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x}\left(A^{n}_{\infty}|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)=A_{s\wedge\sigma}^{n}+\phi_{D_{n}}(\rho_{s\wedge\sigma},W_{s\wedge\sigma})\>\>\>\text{ and }\>\>\>\mathbb{N}_{x}\left(A_{\infty}|\mathcal{F}_{s}\right)=A_{s\wedge\sigma}+\phi_{D}(\rho_{s\wedge\sigma},W_{s\wedge\sigma}),

(3.15)

since
\phi_{D_{n}}(\mu,\text{w})=\mathbbm{E}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\text{w}}[A^{n}_{\infty}]
,
\phi_{D}(\mu,\text{w})=\mathbbm{E}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\text{w}}[A_{\infty}]
and
\phi_{D_{n}}(\rho_{\sigma},W_{\sigma})=\phi_{D}(\rho_{\sigma},W_{\sigma})=0
,
\mathbb{N}_{x}
-a.e. To simplify notation, we denote respectively by
M^{n}_{s}=\mathbb{N}_{x}(A^{n}_{\infty}|\mathcal{F}_{s})
and
M_{s}=\mathbb{N}_{x}(A_{\infty}|\mathcal{F}_{s})
for
s\geq 0
the martingales in (
3.15
). Next, we apply Doob’s inequality to derive:

\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(\sup\limits_{s>0}|M_{s}^{n}-M_{s}|>\delta\big)\leq\delta^{-2}\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(|A^{n}_{\sigma}-A_{\sigma}|^{2}\big).

(3.16)

Indeed, even if
\mathbb{N}_{x}
is not a finite measure, we can argue as follows: fix
a>0
and observe that
(M_{a+t})_{t\geq 0}
,
(M^{n}_{a+t})_{t\geq 0}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x}(\,\cdot\,|\sigma>a)
are uniformly integrable martingales, from which we obtain

\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(\sup\limits_{s\geq a}|M_{s}^{n}-M_{s}|>\delta\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \sigma>a\big)\leq\delta^{-2}\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(|A^{n}_{\sigma}-A_{\sigma}|^{2}\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \sigma>a\big),

and we deduce (
3.16
) by multiplying both sides by
\mathbb{N}_{x}(\sigma>a)
and by taking the limit as
a\downarrow 0
– using monotone convergence.

By (
3.11
), the right-hand side of (
3.16
) converges towards
0
as
n\uparrow\infty
and we deduce that

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\limits_{s>0}|M_{s}^{n_{k}}-M_{s}|=0,\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{ --a.e. }

for a suitable subsequence
(n_{k}:\penalty\ k\geq 1)
increasing towards infinity.
Since
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\phi_{D_{n}}(\rho_{s},W_{s})=\phi_{D}(\rho_{s},W_{s})
, we obtain that
\mathbb{N}_{x}
-a.e., for every
t\geq 0
,
\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n_{k}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\rightarrow\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L^{D}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. By continuity, monotonicity and the fact that
\sigma<\infty

\mathbb{N}_{x}
–a.e., we can apply Dini’s theorem to get:

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t>0}\big|\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D_{n_{k}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}-\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}}\big|=0,\quad\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{-- a.e.}

Consequently, we deduce that on the event
\{\sup_{s\geq 0}\langle\rho_{s},1\rangle\leq K\}=\{\sup X\leq K\}
, the
\mathbb{N}_{x}
-a.e. uniform convergence (
3.10
) holds under a subsequence
(n_{k})
, which depends on
K
. Since this holds for arbitrary
K
, we can use a diagonal argument to find a deterministic subsequence that we still denote by
(n_{k}:k\geq 1)
converging towards infinity such that

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,\sigma]}|L_{t}^{D_{n_{k}}}-L_{t}^{D}|=0,\quad\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{-- a.e.}

∎

We are now in position to prove that the process
\widetilde{L}^{D}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Until further notice, we argue under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
. By (
3.3
) and monotonicity, a diagonal argument gives that we can find a subsequence
(\varepsilon_{k}:\penalty\ k\geq 1)
, with
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, such that:

L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D_{n}}=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})<H_{r}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},

for every
n\geq 1
and
s\geq 0
. Our goal is now to show that:

\displaystyle L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D_{n}}=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{\Gamma_{r}^{D}})<H_{\Gamma^{D}_{r}}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{\Gamma^{D}_{r}})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},

(3.17)

which will imply that
(L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D_{n}})_{s\geq 0}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In order to establish (
3.17
) we argue for
\omega
fixed and observe that for
k
large enough, we have:

\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})<H_{r}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})<H_{r}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{H_{r}\leq\tau_{D}(W_{r})\}},\quad\text{ for all }r\in[0,\Gamma_{s}^{D}].

To see it, remark that if the previous display did not hold, by a compactness argument and continuity we would have
\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r_{0}})=\tau_{D}(W_{r_{0}})\leq H_{r}
for some
r_{0}
in
[0,\Gamma_{s}^{D}]
. This gives a contradiction since
\overline{D}_{n}\subset D
and
(W_{r_{0}}(t))_{t\in[0,H_{r_{0}}]}
is continuous. Recalling the notation
V^{D}
given in (
3.1
), we deduce that

\displaystyle L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})<H_{r}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}{\mathrm{d}}V^{D}_{r}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})<H_{r}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{r})+\varepsilon_{k}\}}=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{\Gamma_{r}^{D}})<H_{{\Gamma_{r}^{D}}}<\tau_{D_{n}}(W_{\Gamma_{r}^{D}})+\varepsilon_{k}\}},

giving us (
3.17
). The same arguments can be applied under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
and, to complete the proof of the proposition, it suffices to show that for every
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,t]}|L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D_{n}}-L_{\Gamma_{s}^{D}}^{D}|=0,\quad\quad\text{ under }\mathbb{P}_{0,x}\text{ and }\mathbb{N}_{x},

(3.18)

at least along a suitable subsequence. However, note that when working under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, this convergence follows by Lemma
4
. Now, the result under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
is a standard consequence of excursion theory. More precisely,
recall that
-I
is the local time of
(\rho,W)
at
(0,x)
and, for fixed
r>0
, set
T_{r}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ -I_{t}>r\}
. If we let
T_{D}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\tau_{D}(W_{t})<\infty\}
, by continuity there exists a finite number of excursions
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
of
(\rho,W)
in
[0,T_{r}]
satisfying
T_{D}(W^{i})<\infty
, and their distribution is
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\,\cdot\,|T_{D}<\infty)
. Since
T_{r}\uparrow\infty
, the approximation (
3.18
) under
\mathbb{P}_{x,0}
now follows from the result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. This completes the proof of Proposition
3
.
∎

3.2
Proof of special Markov property

Now that we have already studied the trajectories staying in
D
, we turn our attention to the complementary side of the picture and we start by introducing formally the notion of excursions from
D
.

Excursions from
D
.
Observe that (
2.20
) and assumption

(

H
1

)

imply that

\mathbb{N}_{x}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}(W_{s})<\zeta_{s}\}}>0\Big)>0.

Hence, the set
\big\{s\in[0,\sigma]:\>\tau_{D}(W_{s})<\zeta_{s}\big\}
is non-empty with non null measure under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
. If we define

\gamma^{D}_{s}:=\big(\zeta_{s}-\tau_{D}(W_{s})\big)_{+},\quad\quad s\geq 0,

it is straightforward to show by the snake property and the continuity of
\zeta
that
\gamma^{D}
is continuous. Set

\sigma^{D}_{t}:=\inf\big\{s\geq 0:\>\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma^{D}_{r}>0\}}>t\big\},

and consider the process
(\rho^{D}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
taking values in
\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
defined by:

\langle\rho^{D}_{t},f\rangle:=\int\rho_{\sigma^{D}_{t}}({\mathrm{d}}h)f\big(h-\tau_{D}(W_{\sigma^{D}_{t}})\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{h>\tau_{D}(W_{\sigma_{t}^{D}})\}}.

(3.19)

Then, by Proposition 4.3.1 in
[
11
]
,
\rho^{D}
and
\rho
have the same distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
. In particular,
\langle\rho^{D},1\rangle
has the same law as the reflected Lévy process
X-I
and we denote its local time at
0
by
(\ell^{D}(s):s\geq 0)
. Moreover, it is shown in
[
11
, Section 4.3]
that the process
L^{D}
is related to the local time
\ell^{D}
by the identity:

L^{D}_{t}=\ell^{D}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma^{D}_{s}>0\}}\right).

(3.20)

The proof of Proposition 4.3.1 in
[
11
]
shows that
\rho^{D}
can be obtained as limit of functions which are independent of
\mathcal{F}^{D}
, implying that
\rho^{D}
is on its turn independent of
\mathcal{F}^{D}
.
Now, denote the connected components of the open set

\big\{t\geq 0:\>\tau_{D}(W_{t})<\zeta_{t}\big\}=\big\{t\geq 0:\gamma^{D}_{t}>0\big\},

by
\big((a_{i},b_{i}):i\in\mathcal{I}\big)
,
where
\mathcal{I}
is an indexing set that might be empty. By construction, for any
s\in(a_{i},b_{i})
, the trajectory
W_{s}
is a trajectory leaving
D
. Remark that
H_{a_{i}}=H_{b_{i}}<H_{r}
for every
r\in(a_{i},b_{i})
and let
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
be the subtrajectory of
(\rho,W)
associated with
[a_{i},b_{i}]
as defined in Section
2.3
. Observe that in our setting,
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
is defined for each
s\in[0,b_{i}-a_{i}]
and for any measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
as

\displaystyle\langle\rho^{i}_{s},f\rangle=\int\rho_{a_{i}+s}({\mathrm{d}}h)f(h-\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}}))\mathbbm{1}_{\{h>\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}})\}}

and

W^{i}_{s}=W_{(a_{i}+s)\wedge b_{i}}(t+\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}}))\quad\text{ for }t\in[0,\zeta_{(a_{i}+s)\wedge b_{i}}-\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}})],

with respective lifetime process given by

\zeta_{s}^{i}=\zeta_{(a_{i}+s)\wedge b_{i}}-\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}}),

where
\tau_{D}(W_{s})=\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}})=\zeta_{a_{i}}
. We say that
(\rho^{i},W^{i})
is an
excursion
of
(\rho,W)
from
D
. Observe that
W_{s}^{i}(0)=W_{a_{i}}^{i}(0)
for all
s\in[a_{i},b_{i}]
by the snake property and that we have
W_{a_{i}}^{i}(0)\in\partial D
. This is the point of
\partial D
used by the subtrajectory
W^{i}
to escape from
D
.

In order to state the special Markov property we need to introduce one last notation. Let
\theta
be the right inverse of
\widetilde{L}^{D}
, viz. the
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable function defined as

\theta_{r}:=\inf\big\{s\geq 0\>:L_{\Gamma^{D}_{s}}^{D}>r\big\},\quad\text{ for all }r\in[0,L_{\sigma}^{D}).\>

Recall that we are considering some fixed
x\in D
, the notation
((\rho^{i},W^{i}):i\in\mathcal{I})
for the excursions outside
D
, and that we are working under the hypothesis

(

H
1

)

. We are now going to state and prove the special Markov property under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
, and we will deduce by standard arguments a version under the excursion measure
\mathbb{N}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
we use the same notation as under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, but observing that
\sigma_{H}=\infty
and noticing that
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
-a.s., we have
\mathcal{Y}_{D}=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{H_{s}\leq\tau_{D}(W_{s})\}}=\infty
and
L^{D}_{\infty}=\infty
. In particular, this implies that
\Gamma^{D}_{s}
and
\theta_{s}
are finite for every
s<\infty
.

Theorem 3.1

(Special Markov property)
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D}
, the point measure

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W^{i})}({\mathrm{d}}\ell,\>{\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\omega)

is a Poisson point process with intensity

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\ell)\>{\mathrm{d}}\ell\>\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{tr}_{D}(\widehat{W})_{\theta_{\ell}}}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\omega).

Recall that we have established in Proposition
3
that
\widetilde{L}^{D}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable. It might also be worth observing that if
F=F(\uprho,\omega)
is a measurable function, when integrating with respect to the intensity measure
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\ell)\>{\mathrm{d}}\ell\>\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{tr}_{D}(\widehat{W})_{\theta_{\ell}}}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\omega)
we can re-write the expression in the following more tractable form:

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\,\mathbb{N}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{\ell}}}(F)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{L}_{s}^{D}\,\mathbb{N}_{\text{tr}_{D}(\widehat{W})_{s}}(F)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}{L}_{s}^{D}\,\mathbb{N}_{\widehat{W}_{s}}(F)

where in the last equality, we applied a change of variable for Lebesgue-Stieltjes integrals using the fact that
L^{D}
is constant on the excursion intervals
[\Gamma^{D}_{s-},\Gamma^{D}_{s}]
when
\Gamma^{D}_{s-}<\Gamma^{D}_{s}
. Let us now prove Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

In this proof, we work with
(\rho,W)
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
. Let us start with some preliminary constructions and remarks. First, we introduce the
\mathcal{S}_{x}
-valued process
(\rho,W^{*})
defined at each
t\geq 0
as

\big(\rho_{t},W^{*}_{t}(s)\big)=\Big(\rho_{t}({\mathrm{d}}h),W_{t}\big(s\wedge\tau_{D}(W_{t})\big)\Big),\quad\text{ for }s\in[0,\zeta_{W_{t}}],

and let
\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
be its generated sigma-field on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E,x})
. The snake
(\rho,W^{*})
can be interpreted as the Lévy snake associated with
(\psi,\xi^{*})
, where
\xi^{*}
is the stopped Markov process
(\xi^{*}_{t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0)=(\xi_{t\wedge\tau_{D}(\xi)}:\penalty\ t\geq 0)
. Since, for every
t\geq 0
,

\big(\zeta_{W_{t}}-\tau_{D}(W_{t})\big)_{+}=\big(\zeta_{W_{t}^{*}}-\tau_{D}(W_{t}^{*})\big)_{+},

we derive that the process
\gamma_{t}^{D}=(\zeta_{W_{t}}-\tau_{D}(W_{t}))_{+}
is
\mathcal{F}_{*}^{D}-
measurable. Consequently, we have
\mathcal{F}^{D}\subset\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
since
V^{D}
– the functional measuring the time spent in
D
defined in (
3.1
) – is
\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
-measurable and by definition
\rm{tr}_{D}(\rho,W)=\rm{tr}_{D}(\rho,W^{*})
. Recalling that
\big((a_{i},b_{i}):i\in\mathcal{I}\big)
stands for the connected components of the open set

\{t\geq 0:\>\tau_{D}(W_{t})<\zeta_{W_{t}}\}=\{t\geq 0:\gamma^{D}_{t}>0\},

we deduce by the previous discussion and the identity
\tau_{D}(W_{a_{i}})=\zeta_{a_{i}}
, that the variables

\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}=\widehat{W}^{*}_{a_{i}},\,\zeta^{i}=\zeta_{(a_{i}+\cdot\,)\wedge b_{i}}-\zeta_{a_{i}}\text{ and a fortiori }\,\rho^{i}\,\text{ are }\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}-\text{ measurable}.

Informally,
\mathcal{F}_{*}^{D}
encodes the information of the trajectories staying in
D
and the tree structure.
We claim that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
, the excursions
(W^{i}:i\in\mathbb{N})
are independent, and that the conditional distribution of
W^{i}
is
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}^{*}}^{\zeta^{i}}
, where we recall from Section
2.3
that we denote the distribution of the snake driven by
h
started at
x
by
\mathbb{Q}^{h}_{x}
.

In order to prove this claim, consider a collection of snake trajectories
\big(W^{i,\prime}:i\in\mathcal{I}\big)
such that, conditionally on
(\rho,W^{*})
, they are independent and each one is respectively distributed according to the measure
\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}^{*}}^{\zeta^{i}}
. Next let
W^{\prime}
be the process defined as follows: for every
t
such that
\gamma_{t}^{D}=0
set
W^{\prime}_{t}=W^{*}_{t}
, and if
\gamma_{t}^{D}>0
we set:

W^{\prime}_{t}(s)=\begin{cases}W_{t}^{*}(s)\hskip 76.82243pt\,\,\text{if}\>\>s\in[0,\tau_{D}(W_{t}^{*})]\\
W_{t-a_{i}}^{i,\prime}\big(s-\tau_{D}(W_{t}^{*})\big)\>\>\hskip 11.38109pt\text{if}\>\>s\in[\tau_{D}(W_{t}^{*}),\zeta(W_{t}^{*})],\end{cases}

where
i
is the unique index such that
t\in(a_{i},b_{i})
. By construction,
(\rho,W^{\prime})
is in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E,x})
and a straightforward computation of its finite marginals shows that its distribution is
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
, proving our claim.

Notice that (
3.3
) implies that
L^{D}
is constant on the intervals
[\Gamma^{D}_{s-},\Gamma^{D}_{s}]
when
\Gamma^{D}_{s-}<\Gamma^{D}_{s}
. Hence,
L^{D}_{s}=\widetilde{L}^{D}_{V^{D}_{s}}
for all
s\geq 0
and in particular
L^{D}_{a_{i}}=\widetilde{L}^{D}_{V^{D}_{a_{i}}}
,
the latter being
\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
-measurable. Consider now
U
a bounded
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable random variable, and remark that to obtain the desired result, it is enough to show that:

\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\exp(-\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}F\big(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W^{i})\big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\>\mathbb{N}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{\ell}}}\big(1-\exp(-F(\ell,\rho,W)\big)\Big)\Big],

for every non-negative measurable function
F
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{E})
. In order to prove this identity, we start by projecting the left term on
\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
: by the previous discussion and recalling that
\mathcal{F}^{D}\subset\mathcal{F}^{D}_{*}
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\exp(-\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}F\big(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W^{i})\big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}^{*}}^{\zeta^{i}}\big(\exp(-F(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W)\big)\Big].

Moreover, it is straightforward to see that

\widehat{W}^{*}_{a_{i}}=\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}=\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{L_{a_{i}}^{D}}},

we omit the details of this identity since the argument used in (23) of
[
22
, Theorem 20]
for the Brownian snake applies directly to our framework.
Consequently, we have:

\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{Q}_{\widehat{W}_{a_{i}}^{*}}^{\zeta^{i}}\big(\exp(-F(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W)\big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{Q}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{L_{a_{i}}^{D}}}}^{\zeta^{i}}\big(\exp(-F(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W)\big)\Big].

Now, we need to take the projection on
\mathcal{F}^{D}
.
Recalling that
H(\rho^{i})=\zeta^{i}
, observe that for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
,

\mathbb{Q}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{L_{a_{i}}^{D}}}}^{\zeta^{i}}\big(\exp(-F(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W)\big)

is a measurable function of the pair
(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i})
and the process
(\text{tr}_{D}(W)_{\theta_{r}}:\penalty\ r\geq 0)
, the latter being
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable. We are going to conclude by showing that the point measure

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
independent of
\mathcal{F}^{D}
. Remark that once this has been established, an application of the exponential formula for functionals of Poisson random measures yields

\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\prod\limits_{i\in\mathcal{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{Q}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{L_{a_{i}}^{D}}}}^{\zeta^{i}}\big(\exp(-F(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W)\big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0,x}\Big[U\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\>\mathbb{N}_{\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{(W)}_{\theta_{\ell}}}\big(1-\exp(-F(\ell,\rho,W)\big)\Big)\Big],

giving the desired result. In this direction, recall the definition of
\rho^{D}
given in (
3.19
), and that
\ell^{D}
stands for the local time of
\rho^{D}
at 0.
We denote the connected component of the open set
\{t\geq 0:\,\langle\rho^{D}_{t},1\rangle\neq 0\}=\{t\geq 0:H(\rho^{D}_{t})>0\}
by
\big((c_{j},d_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\big)
– the latter equality holding since
\rho^{D}_{t}(\{0\})=0
– and observe that these are precisely the excursion intervals of
\langle\rho^{D},1\rangle
from
0
. It follows by (
2.8
) and the discussion before the proof that

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(\ell^{D}(c_{j}),\,\rho^{D}_{(c_{j}+\cdot)\wedge d_{j}})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\,N({\mathrm{d}}\rho)
and observe that this measure is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{D}
– since
\rho^{D}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{D}
.
Furthermore, by (
3.20
) we have:

L_{\sigma_{s}^{D}}^{D}=\ell^{D}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{s}^{D}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma^{D}_{r}>0\}}\Big)=\ell^{D}(s),

for every
s\geq 0
.
It is now straightforward to deduce from our last observations that:

\big\{(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i}):i\in\mathcal{I}\big\}=\big\{(\ell^{D}(c_{j}),\rho^{D}_{(c_{j}+\cdot)\wedge d_{j}}):j\in\mathbb{N}\big\},

concluding the proof.

∎

Setting
T_{D}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\tau_{D}(W_{t})<\infty\}
, we infer from our previous result a version of the special Markov property holding under the probability measure

\mathbb{N}^{D}_{x}:=\mathbb{N}_{x}(\,\cdot\,|T_{D}<\infty).

Observe that
\mathbb{N}_{x}(T_{D}<\infty)
is finite: if this quantity was infinite, by excursion theory, the process
(\rho,W)
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x}
would have infinitely many excursions exiting
D
on compact intervals, contradicting the continuity of its paths. Finally, note that
(\rho,W)
under
\mathbb{N}^{D}_{x}
has the distribution of the first excursion exiting the domain
D
. As a straightforward consequence of Theorem
3.1
, this observation allows us to deduce:

Theorem 3.2
.

Under
\mathbb{N}^{D}_{x}
and conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D}
, the point measure:

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(L_{a_{i}}^{D},\rho^{i},W^{i})}({\mathrm{d}}\ell,\>{\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\omega)

is a Poisson point process with intensity

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,L_{\sigma}^{D}]}(\ell)\>{\mathrm{d}}\ell\>\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{tr}_{D}(\widehat{W})_{\theta_{\ell}}}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\omega).

Recall that the measure
{\mathrm{d}}L_{s}^{D}
is supported on
\{s\geq 0:\widehat{W}_{s}\in\partial D\}
and consider a measurable function
g:\partial D\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Under
\mathbb{N}_{x}
, we define the
exit measure from
D
, denoted by
\mathcal{Z}^{D}
as:

\langle\mathcal{Z}^{D},g\rangle:=\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}L^{D}_{s}g(\widehat{W}_{s}).

The total mass of
\mathcal{Z}^{D}
is
L_{\sigma}^{D}
and, in particular,
\mathcal{Z}^{D}
is non-null only in
\{T_{D}<\infty\}
. Again by a standard change of variable, we get

\langle\mathcal{Z}^{D},g\rangle=\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{L}^{D}_{s}g(\text{tr}_{D}(\widehat{W}_{s}))=\int_{0}^{L^{D}_{\sigma}}{\mathrm{d}}\ell\,g({\text{tr}_{D}\widehat{W}_{\theta_{\ell}}}),\quad\quad\mathbb{N}_{x}\text{--a.e.}

and this implies that
\mathcal{Z}^{D}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D}
-measurable since
L^{D}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}^{D}
by Proposition
3
. In this work, we shall frequently make use of the following simpler version of the special Markov property. By Theorem
3.2
, we have

Corollary 1
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x}^{D}
and conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D}
, the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(\rho^{i},W^{i})}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\,{\mathrm{d}}\omega)

(3.21)

is a Poisson random measure with intensity
\int\mathcal{Z}^{D}({\mathrm{d}}y)\mathbb{N}_{y}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\,{\mathrm{d}}\omega)
.

Let us close this section by recalling some well-known properties of

\mathcal{Z}^{D}
that will be needed, and by introducing some useful notations. Remark by (
3.4
) that, for any measurable
g:\partial D\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
and for every
y\in D
, we have

\>\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(\langle\mathcal{Z}^{D},g\rangle\big)=\Pi_{y}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}\exp(-\alpha\tau_{D})g(\xi_{\tau_{D}})\Big),

and for such
g
, we set:

\>u_{g}^{D}(y):=\mathbb{N}_{y}\big(1-\exp(-\langle\mathcal{Z}^{D},g\rangle)\big),\quad\text{ for all }y\in D.

(3.22)

Theorem 4.3.3 in
[
11
]

states that for every
g:\partial D\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
bounded measurable function,
u_{g}^{D}
solves the integral equation:

u_{g}^{D}(y)+\Pi_{y}\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\psi(u_{g}^{D}(\xi_{t}))\Big)=\Pi_{y}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{D}<\infty\}}g(\xi_{\tau_{D}})\big).

(3.23)

By convention, we set
u_{g}^{D}(y):=g(y)
for every
y\in\partial D
, and we stress that this convention is compatible with (
3.23
).

4
Construction of a measure supported on

\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}

From now on, we fix
x\in E
and we consider the random set:

\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\},\quad\text{ as well as its image on the tree $\mathcal{T}_{H}$, viz. }\quad\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}:\xi_{\upsilon}=x\}.

(4.1)

In order to study the latter, we shall construct an additive functional
A:=(A_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of the Lévy snake supported on
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}
. The present section is devoted to the construction of
A
and to develop the machinery needed for our analysis.
The study of
\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}:\xi_{\upsilon}=x\}
is delayed to Section
5
and will heavily rely on the results of this section. Let us discuss now in detail the framework we will consider in the rest of this work.

Framework of Section
4
and
5
:
With the same notations as in previous sections, consider a strong Markov process
\xi
taking values in
E
with a.s. continuous sample paths and we make the following assumptions:

\bm{x}\>\>\textbf{is regular, instantaneous and recurrent for}\>\>{\xi},

\mathbf{(H_{2})}

and

\mathbf{\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}t\>\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{t}=\bm{x}\}}=0},\quad\quad\Pi_{x}-\text{a.s. }

\mathbf{(H_{3})}

Under

(

H
2

)

the local time of
\xi
at
x
is well defined up to a multiplicative constant (that we fix arbitrarily) and we denote it by
\mathcal{L}
. The recurrence hypothesis is assumed for convenience and we expect our results to hold with minor modifications without it.
Set
E_{*}:=E\setminus\{x\}
and for
{\text{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
, with the notation of Section
3
write

\tau_{E_{*}}({\text{w}})=\inf\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\text{w}}]:{\text{w}}(h)=x\},

for the exit time of
w
from the open set
E_{*}
. Observe that since
x
is recurrent for
\xi
, we have

\Pi_{y}(\tau_{E_{*}}<\infty)=1

(4.2)

for every
y\in E_{*}
, and in particular

(

H
1

)

holds. This will allow us to make use of the special Markov property established in the previous section. Assumption

(

H
3

)

might seem a technicality but it plays a crucial role in our study: it will ensure, under
\mathbb{N}_{y}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,y}
, that the set of branching points of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
and
\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}\setminus\{0\}:\xi_{\upsilon}=x\}
are disjoint. We will explain properly this point after concluding the presentation of the section.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the excursion measure of
\xi
at
x
associated with
\mathcal{L}
and, with a slight abuse of notation, still write
\sigma_{\xi}
for the lifetime of
\xi
under
\mathcal{N}
. The pair

\overline{\xi}_{s}=(\xi_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}),\quad s\geq 0,

is a strong Markov process taking values in the Polish space
\overline{E}:=E\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
equipped with the product metric
d_{\overline{E}}
. We set
\Pi_{y,r}
for its law started from an arbitrary point
(y,r)\in\overline{E}
. Recall that we always work under the assumptions

(

H
0

)

, which for
(\psi,\overline{\xi})
takes the following form:

Hypothesis
(\textbf{H}^{\prime}_{0})
. There exists a constant
C_{\Pi}>0
and two positive numbers
p,q>0
such that,

for every
y\in E
and
t\geq 0
, we have:

\hskip-42.67912pt\Pi_{y,0}\Big(\sup_{0\leq u\leq t}d_{\overline{E}}\big((\xi_{u},\mathcal{L}_{u}),(y,0)\big)^{p}\Big)\leq C_{\Pi}\cdot t^{q},\hskip 19.91692pt\text{ and }\hskip 19.91692ptq\cdot(1-\Upsilon^{-1})>1,

\mathbf{(H_{0}^{\prime})}

where we recall the definition of
\Upsilon
from (
2.13
).
We will use respectively the notation
\overline{\Theta}
,
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
for the sets defined as
\Theta
,
\mathcal{S}
in Section
2.3
but replacing the Polish space
E
by
\overline{E}
. It will be convenient to write the elements of
\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
as pairs
\overline{\text{w}}=(\text{w},\ell)
, where
{\text{w}}\in\mathcal{W}_{E}
and
\ell:[0,\zeta_{\text{w}}]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous function.
Recall that under

(

H
0

′

)

, the family of measures
(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}:(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta})
are defined in the canonical space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
and we denote the canonical process by
(\rho,W,\Lambda)
, where
W_{s}:[0,\zeta_{s}(\overline{W}_{s})]\mapsto E
and
\Lambda_{s}:[0,\zeta_{s}(\overline{W}_{s})]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Said otherwise, for each
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}
, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
the process

(\rho_{s},W_{s},\Lambda_{s}),\quad s\geq 0,

is the
\psi
-Lévy snake with spatial motion
\overline{\xi}
started from
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})
and
we simply write
\overline{W}_{s}:=(W_{s},\Lambda_{s})
. For every
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
, we denote the excursion measure of
(\rho,\overline{W})
starting from
(0,y,r_{0})
by
{\mathbb{N}}_{y,r_{0}}
.

Recall that under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
or
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
, for each
s\geq 0
and conditionally on
\zeta_{s}
, the pair

(W_{s},\Lambda_{s})=\big((W_{s}(h),\Lambda_{s}(h)\big):h\in[0,\zeta_{s}]\big)

has the distribution of
(\xi,\mathcal{L})
under
\Pi_{y,r_{0}}
killed at
\zeta_{s}
. In particular, the associated Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure of
\Lambda_{s}
is supported on the closure of
\{h\in[0,\zeta_{s}):W_{s}(h)=x\}
,
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e. We will restrict our analysis to the collection of initial conditions
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}):=(\mu,{\text{w}},\ell)\in\overline{\Theta}
satisfying that:

(i)

\ell
is a non-decreasing continuous function and the support of its Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure is

\overline{\big\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\text{w}}):{\text{w}}(h)=x\big\}}.

(ii)

The measure
\mu
does not charge the set
\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\text{w}}]:{\text{w}}(h)=x\}
, viz.

\int_{[0,\zeta_{{\text{w}}}]}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\text{w}}(h)=x\}}=0.

This subcollection of
\overline{\Theta}
is denoted by
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and we will work with the process
\big((\rho,\overline{W}),(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}:(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x})\big)
. Conditions (i) and (ii) are natural, since as a particular consequence of the next lemma, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
the Lévy snake
(\rho,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
.

Lemma 5
.

For every
(\mu,\overline{\emph{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
, the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
.

Proof.

First, we argue that
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e. the pair
(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})
satisfies (i) and (ii) for each
t\in[0,\sigma]
.
On the one hand, by formula (
2.20
), for every
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
we have :

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma_{H}}{\mathrm{d}}t\,\langle\rho_{t},\{h\in[0,H_{t}]:\>W_{t}(h)=x\}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}a\>\exp(-\alpha a)\>E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{y,r_{0}}\big[\int_{0}^{a}J_{a}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{h}=x\}}\big]=0.

In the last equality we used that, by

(

H
3

)

and the independence between
\xi
and
J_{\infty}
, Campbell’s formula yields

E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{y,r_{0}}\big[\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{h}=x\}}\>\big]=0
. On the other hand, by construction of the Lévy snake, for each fixed
t\geq 0
the support of
\Lambda_{t}({\mathrm{d}}h)
is the closure of
\{h\in[0,H_{t}):W_{t}(h)=x\}
in
[0,H_{t}]
,
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e.

Consequently,
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e. , we can find a countable dense set
\mathcal{D}\subset[0,\sigma]
such that we have

\langle\rho_{t},\{h\in[0,H_{t}]:\>W_{t}(h)=x\}\rangle=0
and
\text{supp }\Lambda_{t}({\mathrm{d}}h)
is the closure of
\{h\in[0,H_{t}):W_{t}(h)=x\}
,

for every
t\in\mathcal{D}
. For instance, one can construct the set
\mathcal{D}
by taking an infinite sequence of independent uniform points in
[0,\sigma]
. We now claim that
\rho
satisfies that
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e., for every
s<t
, we have
\rho_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,m_{H}(s,t))}=\rho_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,m_{H}(s,t))}
, where we recall the notation
m_{H}(s,t)=\min_{[s,t]}H
. Indeed, remark that for fixed
s<t
, this holds by the Markov property and we can extend this property to all
0\leq s<t\leq\sigma
since
\rho
is right-continuous with respect to the total variation distance. Now, by the snake property we deduce that
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
–a.e, for every
t\in[0,\sigma]
, we have

\langle\rho_{t},\{h\in[0,H_{t}):\>W_{t}(h)=x\}\rangle=0\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\{h\in[0,H_{t}):{W}_{t}(h)=x\}=\text{supp }\Lambda_{t}({\mathrm{d}}h)\cap[0,H_{t}).

(4.3)

Taking the closure in the second equality we deduce that the closure of
\{h\in[0,H_{t}):{W}_{t}(h)=x\}
is exactly
\text{supp }\Lambda_{t}({\mathrm{d}}h)
. However, to conclude that
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
-a.e.

\langle\rho_{t},\{h\in[0,H_{t}]:\>W_{t}(h)=x\}\rangle=0,\quad\quad\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,\sigma],

(4.4)

we still need an additional step. Arguing by contradiction, suppose that for some
t>0
the quantity (
4.4
) is non-null. Then, by (
4.3
) we must have
\rho_{t}(\{H_{t}\})>0
and
W_{t}(H_{t})=x
. By right-continuity of
\rho
with respect to the total variation metric, we get

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}|\rho_{t}(\{H_{t}\})-\rho_{t+\varepsilon}(\{H_{t}\})|=0,

and we deduce that for
\varepsilon
small enough,
\rho_{u}(\{H_{t}\})>0
for all
u\in[t,t+\varepsilon)
; in particular
H(\rho_{u})\geq H(\rho_{t})
for all
u\in[t,t+\varepsilon)
. Since
W_{t}(H_{t})=x
, the snake property ensures that
{W}_{u}(H_{t})=x
for all
u\in[t,t+\varepsilon)
and, since
\rho_{u}(\{H_{t}\})>0
for every
u\in[t,t+\varepsilon)
, we obtain a contradiction with the fact that
\langle\rho_{u},\{h\in[0,H_{u}]:\>W_{u}(h)=x\}\rangle=0
for every
u\in\mathcal{D}
.

Let us now deduce this result under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
. First, observe that the statement of the lemma follows directly under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
by excursion theory. Next, fix
(\mu,\overline{\text{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
with
\overline{{\text{w}}}(0)=(y,r_{0})
, consider
(\rho,\overline{W})
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
and set
T_{0}^{+}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\>\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\}
. The strong Markov property gives us that
((\rho_{T_{0}^{+}+t},\overline{W}_{T_{0}^{+}+t}):\penalty\ t\geq 0)
is distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
and consequently,
(\rho_{T_{0}^{+}+t},\overline{W}_{T_{0}^{+}+t})_{t\geq 0}
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. To conclude, it remains to prove the statement of the lemma under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
. In this direction, under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
, consider
\big((\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):\penalty\ i\geq 0\big)
the excursion intervals of
\langle\rho,1\rangle
from its running infimum. Then, write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
for the subtrajectories of
(\rho,\overline{W})
associated with
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
and set
h_{i}:=H_{\alpha_{i}}
. We recall from (
2.18
) that the measure:

\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})},

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\>\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\>{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}).
Since
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, it follows by the result under the excursion measures
(\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}:(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E})
that
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
–a.s. the pair
(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})
belongs to
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
for every
t\in[0,T_{0}^{+}]
, as wanted.
∎

Finally, recall that the snake property ensures that the function
(\widehat{W}_{s},\widehat{\Lambda}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
is well defined in the quotient space
\mathcal{T}_{H}
. Hence, we can think of
\overline{W}
as a tree-indexed process, that we write with the usual abuse of notation as

(\xi_{\upsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}.

Main results of Section 4:
Now that we have introduced our framework, we can state the main results of this section. Much of our effort is devoted to the construction of a measure supported on the set
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}
and satisfying suitable properties. In this direction, for every
r\geq 0
, we set
\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}):=\inf\{h\geq 0:\penalty\ \overline{{\text{w}}}(h)=(x,r)\}
and remark that, for every
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, it holds that
\mu(\{\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})\})=0,
with the convention
\mu(\infty)=0
. We can now state the main result of this section:

Theorem 4.1
.

Fix
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
and
(\mu,\overline{\emph{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
.
The convergence

A_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}},

(4.5)

holds uniformly in compact intervals in measure under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}(\,\cdot\,\cap\{\sigma>z\})
for every
z>0
. Moreover, (
4.5
) defines a continuous additive functional
A=(A_{t})
for the Lévy snake
(\rho,\overline{W})
whose Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
is supported on
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}
.

We will give another equivalent construction of the additive functional
A
in Proposition
6
but we are not yet in position to formulate the precise statement. Both constructions will be needed for our work. Next, the second main result of the section characterizes the support of the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
as follows:

Theorem 4.2
.

Fix
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
,
(\mu,\overline{\emph{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
and
denote the support of the Stieltjets measure of
A
by
\emph{supp}{\mathrm{d}}A
. Under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{{\text{w}}}}}
, we have:

\emph{supp}\,{\mathrm{d}}A=\overline{\big\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\,\xi_{p_{H}(t)}=x\emph{and}p_{H}(t)\in\emph{Multi}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{H})\cup\{0\}\big\}}.

(4.6)

Observe that under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
with
{\text{w}}(0)=x
, the root of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
has infinite multiplicity and this is why we had to consider it separately in the previous display. This result is stated in a slightly different but equivalent form in Theorem
4.3
. The identity (
4.6
) can be also formulated in terms of constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}
. More precisely, we will also establish in Theorem
4.3
that under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
, we have:

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A=[0,\sigma]\setminus\big\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ \sup_{(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)}\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}=\inf_{(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)}\widehat{\Lambda}_{s},\quad\text{ for some }\varepsilon>0\big\}.

(4.7)

We conclude the presentation of our framework with a consequence of Lemma
5
. Roughly speaking it states that, with the exception of the root under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, the process
(\xi_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}
can not take the value
x
at the branching points of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
. The precise statement is the following:

Proposition 4
.

For every
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
and
(\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, we have:

\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}\cap\{t\in[0,\sigma]:p_{H}(t)\in\emph{Multi}_{3}(\mathcal{T}_{H})\cup\emph{Multi}_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{H}),\,{p}_{H}(t)\neq 0\}=\emptyset,

under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
.

Proof.

We start by proving our result under
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}
. First, introduce the measure
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W},\,{\mathrm{d}}s)
supported on
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})\,{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{s\leq\sigma\}}
and write
U:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
for the identity function
U(s)=s
. The law under
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
of
(\rho,\overline{W},U)
is therefore given by

\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}\Big(\Phi(\rho,\overline{W},U)\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\Phi(\rho,\overline{W},s)\Big).

The measure
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
can be seen as a pointed version of
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}
. In particular, conditionally on
(\rho,\overline{W})
, the random variable
U
is a uniform point in
[0,\sigma]
. Under
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}^{\bullet}
we still write
X_{t}:=\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle
and
H_{t}:=H(\rho_{t})
. Furthermore, we set
X^{\bullet}_{t}:=X_{U+t}-X_{U}
and
I^{\bullet}_{t}:=\inf_{s\leq t}X^{\bullet}
, for every
t\geq 0
, and we denote the excursion intervals over the running infimum of
X^{\bullet}
by
\big((\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):\,i\in\mathbb{N}\big)
. The dependence on
U
is dropped to simplify notation. Finally, set

\displaystyle h_{i}^{\bullet}:=H\big(\kappa_{-I^{\bullet}_{\alpha_{i}}}\rho_{U}\big),

and write
(\rho^{\bullet,i},\overline{W}^{\bullet,i})
for the corresponding subtrajectory associated with
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
occurring at height
h_{i}^{\bullet}
. Under
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
, the Markov property applied at time
U
and (
2.18
) gives that, conditionally on
(\rho_{U},W_{U})
, the random measure

\mathcal{\mathcal{M}}^{\bullet}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{({h_{i}^{\bullet},\,\rho^{\bullet,i},\,\overline{W}^{\bullet,i}})},

is a Poisson point measure with intensity

\rho_{U}({\mathrm{d}}h)\,\mathbb{N}_{\overline{W}_{U}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}{W}).
In particular, the functional

F(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})=\#\Big\{(h_{i}^{\bullet},\rho^{\bullet,i},\overline{W}^{\bullet,i})\in\mathcal{M}^{\bullet}:W^{\bullet,i}(0)=x\Big\},

conditionally on
(\rho_{U},W_{U})
, is a Poisson random variable with parameter
\int\rho_{U}(dh)\mathbbm{1}_{\{W_{U}(h)=x\}}
. However, by Lemma
5
, we have

\int\rho_{U}(dh)\mathbbm{1}_{\{W_{U}(h)=x\}}=0
and we derive that,
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
–a.e.,
F(\mathcal{M}^{\bullet})
is null. Heuristically, the previous argument shows that if we take – conditionally on
\sigma
– a point uniformly at random in
\mathcal{T}_{H}
, there is no branching point
\upsilon
with label
x
on the right of the branch connecting the root to
\upsilon
. Let us now show that this ensures that

\big\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\big\}\cap\big\{t\in[0,\sigma]:p_{H}(t)\in\text{Multi}_{3}(\mathcal{T}_{H})\cup\text{Multi}_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{H})\big\}=\emptyset,\quad\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\text{--a.e.}

Since
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}^{\bullet}(\Phi(\rho,\overline{W}))=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}(\sigma\cdot\Phi(\rho,\overline{W}))
, it suffices to prove the previous display under
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
. Let
(\upsilon_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N})
be an indexation of the branching points of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
– an indexation measurable with respect to
H
.
Pick a branching point
\upsilon_{i}\in\text{Multi}_{3}(\mathcal{T}_{H})\cup\text{Multi}_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{H})
and let
t_{i}
be the smallest element of
p_{H}^{-1}(\upsilon_{i})
. Arguing by contradiction, suppose that
\widehat{W}_{p_{H}(t_{i})}=x
. Still under
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
, since
\upsilon_{i}
is a branching point, we can find
0\leq s_{*}<t_{*}\leq\sigma
in
p_{H}^{-1}(\{\upsilon_{i}\})
such that the event

\{\widehat{W}_{p_{H}(t_{i})}=x\}\cap\{s_{*}<U<t_{*}\},

is included in
\{F(\mathcal{M}^{U})>0\}
.
However
F(\mathcal{M}^{U})=0,\,\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}
–a.e. and we deduce that
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}\big(\widehat{W}_{p_{H}(t_{i})}=x,s_{*}<U<t_{*}\big)=0
. Next, since conditionally on
(\rho,\overline{W})
, the variable
U
is uniformly distributed on
[0,\sigma]
, we conclude that
\mathbb{N}^{\bullet}_{y,0}\big(\widehat{W}_{p_{H}(t_{i})}=x\big)=0
. The desired result now follows, since the collection of branching points
(\upsilon_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N})
is countable. Finally, we deduce the statement under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
by the translation invariance of the local time and under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
by excursion theory – we omit the details since this is standard and one can apply the method described in Lemma
5
.
∎

The section is organised as follows:
In Section
4.1
we address several preliminary results needed to prove Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
and our results of Section
5
. More precisely, Section
4.1
is essentially devoted to the study of a family of exit local times of
(\rho,\overline{W})
that will be used as building block for our second construction of
A
. Then in Section
4.2
we shall prove Theorem
4.1
and establish our second construction of
A
in terms of the family of exit times studied in Section
4.1
. The rest of the section is dedicated to the study of basic properties of
A
that we will frequently use in our computations. Finally, in Section
4.3
we turn our attention to the study of the support of the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
and it will lead us to the proof of Theorem
4.2
and the characterisation (
4.7
).

4.1
Special Markov property of the local time

The first step towards constructing our additive functional
A
, with associated Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
supported in
\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}
, consists in the study of a particular family of
[0,\infty)
-indexed exit local times of
(\rho,\overline{W})
. More precisely, for each
r\geq 0
, let
D_{r}\subset\overline{E}:=E\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the open domain

D_{r}:=\overline{E}\setminus\{(x,r)\}\quad\text{ and recall the notation }\quad\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}):=\inf\{h\geq 0:\penalty\ \overline{{\text{w}}}(h)=(x,r)\},

for every
\overline{{\text{w}}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
.
Notice that
\mathcal{\tau}_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})
is the exit time from
D_{r}
and we write
\mathcal{\tau}_{r}
instead of making use of the more cumbersome notation
\mathcal{\tau}_{D_{r}}
. We also recall that since
\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\{h\in[0,\zeta_{\text{w}}]:{\text{w}}(h)=x\}
as soon as
\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})<\infty
, for
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
we have
\mu(\{\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})\})=0
. Proposition
2
now yields that for any
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
with
\overline{{\text{w}}}(0)\neq(x,r)
we have

L^{D_{r}}_{t}(\rho,\overline{W}):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})+\varepsilon\}},

(4.8)

where the convergence holds uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}})
and
L^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(0)})
. Let us be more precise: recalling the notation
\overline{{\text{w}}}=({\text{w}},\ell)
,
first remark that if
\ell(0)<r
, for any
{\text{w}}(0)\in E
we have
\Pi_{{\text{w}}(0),\ell(0)}(\tau_{r}<\infty)=1
and in consequence

(

H
1

)

holds. On the other hand, if
r<\ell(0)
, we simply have
L^{D_{r}}=0
since
\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})=\infty
for every
s\geq 0
. Finally, if
{\text{w}}(0)\neq x
and
r\geq 0
, we have
\tau_{D_{r}}(\overline{W}_{s})=\tau_{E_{*}}(W_{s})
for every
s\geq 0
, and recalling (
4.2
) it follows that
L^{D_{r}}(\rho,\overline{W})=L^{E_{*}}(\rho,W)
. It will be usefully for our purposes to extend the definition to the remaining case
\overline{{\text{w}}}(0)=(x,r)
, and that is precisely the content of the following lemma:

Lemma 6
.

For
r\geq 0
, fix
(\mu,\overline{\rm{w}})=(\mu,\rm{w},\ell)

\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
with
\overline{\rm{w}}(0)=(x,r)
. Then, the limit (
4.8
)
exists for every
t\geq 0
, where the convergence holds uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}})
and
L^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{x,r})
, and defines a continuous non-decreasing process that we still denote by
L^{D_{r}}
. Moreover, under
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
, we have
L^{D_{r}}_{\sigma}=0
.

Proof.

We work under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}}
since the result under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
follows directly by excursion theory.
For every
a\geq 0
we set
T_{a}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ H_{t}=a\}
and let
T_{0}^{+}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\penalty\ \langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\}
. Since
\mathcal{\tau}_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})=0
for every
s\geq 0
, we have

\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})+\varepsilon\}}=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}=\int_{T_{\varepsilon}\wedge t}^{T_{0}^{+}\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}+\int_{T_{0}^{+}\wedge t}^{T_{0}^{+}\wedge t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}.

Furthermore, by the strong Markov property and (
2.5
), we already know that
\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{T_{0}^{+}}^{T_{0}^{+}+t}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}
converges as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly in compact intervals in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\text{w}})
. To conclude, it suffices to show that:

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\big[\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{T_{0}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}\big]=0.

(4.9)

Write
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
the excursion intervals of the killed process
(\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle:\penalty\ t\in[0,T_{0}^{+}])
over its running infimum and let
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
be the subtrajectory associated with the excursion interval
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
. To simplify notation, we also set
h_{i}=H(\alpha_{i})
and recall from (
2.18
) that the measure
\mathcal{M}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. Next, notice that:

\displaystyle\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{T_{0}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}\leq\sum\limits_{0\leq h_{i}\leq\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s}^{i})<\varepsilon\}},

and we can now use that
\mathcal{M}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
to get that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\big[\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{T_{0}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}\big]\leq\mu([0,\varepsilon])N(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s})<\varepsilon\}}).

Finally, by the many-to-one formula (
2.20
), the previous display is

\varepsilon\cdot\mu([0,\varepsilon])
, which gives:

\limsup\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,{\text{w}}}\Big[\int_{T_{\varepsilon}}^{T_{0}^{+}}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H_{s}<\varepsilon\}}\Big]=\mu(\{0\}).

Now (
4.9
) follows since we have
\mu(\{0\})=0
, which holds since
{\text{w}}(0)=x
and
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
.

∎

Now, we give a regularity result for the double-indexed family
({L}_{s}^{D_{r}}:(s,r)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2})
that will be needed in the next section.

Lemma 7
.

Let
(\mu,\overline{\rm{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
with
\overline{\rm{w}}=(\rm{w},\ell)
. There exists a
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{F}
-measurable function

(\mathscr{L}_{t}^{r}:\,(r,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2})
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

For every
r\geq 0
, the processes
{L}^{D_{r}}
and
\mathscr{L}^{r}
are indistinguishables under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}}
.

(ii)

\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}}
almost surely, the mapping
t\mapsto\mathscr{L}^{r}_{t}
is continuous for every
r\geq 0
.

The result also holds under the measure
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
, for every
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
, by the same type of arguments and we omit the details.

Proof.

Fix an initial condition
(\mu,\overline{\rm{w}})=(\mu,{\text{w}},\ell)\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Since under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\text{w}},\ell}
, the distribution of
(\rho,W,\Lambda-\ell(0))
is exactly
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,{\text{w}},\ell-\ell(0)}
, without loss of generality we might assume that
\ell(0)=0
.
Next, by (
4.8
) and Lemma
6
, for every
r\geq 0
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{\rm{w}}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|{L}^{D_{r}}_{s}-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}|\Big]=0,

and hence for any subsequence
(\varepsilon_{n})
converging to
0
, the sequence of processes

Y_{n}(r,t):=\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon_{n}\}},\quad\quad t\geq 0,

converges uniformly in compact intervals in probability towards
{L}^{D_{r}}
. Now, to simplify notation write
\bm{\omega}:=(\uprho,\omega)
for the elements of
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
. Remark now that the mapping
(u,r,\bm{\omega})\mapsto\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u}(\omega))
is jointly measurable since for each
(u,\bm{\omega})
it is càdlàg in
r
, while for each fixed
r
the mapping
(u,\bm{\omega})\mapsto\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u}(\omega))
is measurable. Consequently, by Fubini, for every fixed
t
the application

(r,\bm{\omega})\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon_{n}\}}(\omega)

is measurable while for fixed
(r,\bm{\omega})
it is continuous in
t
, and we deduce that
Y_{n}
is jointly measurable in
(r,t,\bm{\omega})
. It is now standard – see e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 62]
and its proof – to deduce that there exits a jointly measurable process
(r,t,\bm{\omega})\mapsto Y(r,t,\bm{\omega})
such that for every
(r,\bm{\omega})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
, the mapping
t\mapsto Y(r,t,\bm{\omega})
is continuous and for each fixed
r\geq 0
,
Y_{n}(r,\cdot)\mapsto Y(r,\cdot)
as
n\uparrow\infty
uniformly in compact intervals in probability. In particular for each
r\geq 0
, the process
(Y(r,t):t\geq 0)
is indistinguishable from
({L}^{D_{r}}_{t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0)
and we shall write
(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{t}:\penalty\ t\geq 0,\,r\geq 0)
instead of
(Y(r,t):\,t\geq 0,\,r\geq 0)
.
∎

We now turn our attention to the Markovian properties of
(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}:r\geq 0)
under the excursion measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. To simplify notation, for every
y\neq x
, we set:

u_{\lambda}(y):=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\left(1-\exp(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma})\right),\quad\text{ for }y\in E_{*},

(4.10)

and remark that with the notation of (
3.22
) we have
u_{\lambda}(y)=u_{\lambda}^{E_{*}}(y)
. We shall use the usual convention

u_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda
.

Before stating our next result, we briefly recall from
[
20
, Chapter II-1]
that an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
–valued Markov process with semigroup
(P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z):\penalty\ t,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+})
is called a branching process if its semigroup satisfies the branching property, viz. if for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have
P_{t}(y,\cdot)*P_{t}(y^{\prime},\cdot)=P_{t}(y+y^{\prime},\cdot)
. In order to fall in the framework of
[
20
, Chapter II- Theorem 1]
we also assume that
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)z\leq y
for every
t,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By the branching property it follows that for any
t,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
the distribution
P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)
is infinitely divisible and non-negative, and consequently, for every
t,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the Laplace transform of
P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)
