takes the Lévy-Khintchine form:

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\,P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)\exp(-\lambda z)=\exp\big(-ya_{t}(\lambda)\big),\quad\quad\text{ for }\lambda\geq 0,

for some function
(a_{t}(\lambda):t,\lambda\geq 0)
.
By
[
20
, Chapter II- Theorem 1]
, the function
(a_{t}(\lambda):t,\lambda\geq 0)
is the unique non-negative solution of the integral equation

a_{t}(\lambda)+\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\,\Psi(a_{u}(\lambda))=\lambda,

(4.11)

for a function
(\Psi(\lambda):\lambda\geq 0)
of the form,

\Psi(\lambda)=c_{1}\lambda+c_{2}\lambda^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\nu({\mathrm{d}}y)\,(\exp(-\lambda y)-1+\lambda y),\quad\quad\text{ for }\lambda\geq 0,

where
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\nu
is a Lévy measure supported on
(0,\infty)
satisfying
\int_{(0,\infty)}\nu({\mathrm{d}}y)(y\wedge y^{2})<\infty
. By (
4.11
), it follows that
a_{t}(\lambda)
is the unique function that satisfies

\int_{a_{t}(\lambda)}^{\lambda}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}s}{\Psi(s)}=t,\quad\quad t,\lambda\geq 0.

The Markov process with semigroup
(P_{t})
is then called a CSBP with branching mechanism
\Psi
, or in short a
\Psi
-CSBP. The exponent
\Psi
clearly fulfils (A1) and so does (A3) by
[
4
, Corollary 2]
. So to fall in our framework, it only need to satisfy (A4) – since as explained in the preliminaries (A4) implies (A2).

Proposition 5
.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the process
(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}:r>0)
is a branching process with entrance measure
\nu_{r}({\mathrm{d}}x)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\in{\mathrm{d}}x)
, for
r>0
, and branching mechanism

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=\mathcal{N}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \psi\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)\Big),\quad\text{ for }\,\lambda\geq 0.

(4.12)

Moreover,
\widetilde{\psi}
satisfies the assumptions
(A1)
–
(A4)
introduced in Section
2.1
and consequently we can associate to it a Lévy tree.

Our result is a particular case, in the terminology of Lévy snakes, of Theorem 4 in
[
5
]
stated in the setting of superprocesses. Theorem 4 in
[
5
]
is more general and the family
(\mathscr{L}^{r}_{\sigma})_{r>0}
in our result correspond precisely to the total mass process of the superprocess considered in
[
5
]
, for the same branching mechanism
\widetilde{\psi}
.

Proof.

The proof is structured as follows: we start by introducing a family of probability kernels
(P_{t})
and by showing that they form a semigroup of operators associated with a branching process. We then establish that
(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}:\penalty\ r>0)
is a Markov process associated with the semigroup
(P_{t})
, with entrance measure
(\nu_{r}:\,r>0)
. Finally, we conclude the proof by establishing that its branching mechanism is
\widetilde{\psi}
and that it fulfils (A4).

We stress that we are only interested in the finite-dimensional distributions of
(\mathscr{L}^{r}_{\sigma}:r>0)
. Recalling the notation (
3.22
), for any
r>0
and
\lambda\geq 0
, we write

u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}{(x,0)}=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\big)=\int\nu_{r}({\mathrm{d}}y)\,(1-\exp(-\lambda y)).

Moreover, since
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}>0)\leq\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\sup\widehat{\Lambda}\geq r)<\infty
, we have
\int_{(0,\infty)}\nu_{r}({\mathrm{d}}y)(1\wedge y)<\infty
, and we deduce that the function
\lambda\mapsto u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)
is the Laplace exponent of an infinitely divisible random variable with Lévy measure
\nu_{r}(\cdot\cap(0,\infty))
. For each
t>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
denote by
P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)
the probability measure with Laplace transform

\int P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)\,\exp(-\lambda z)=\exp\big(-y\cdot u_{\lambda}^{D_{t}}(x,0)\big),\quad\quad\lambda\geq 0.

(4.13)

Remark now that the translation invariance of the local time of
\xi
implies that, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,r}
(resp.
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
) for
r\geq 0
, the distribution of
(W,\Lambda-r)
is
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
(resp.
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
). In particular, for every
s,t\geq 0
, we have

u_{\lambda}^{D_{t+s}}(x,s)=u_{\lambda}^{D_{t}}(x,0).

We deduce that the family
(P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z),t>0,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+})
is a semigroup since, by the special Markov property applied at the domain
D_{s}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int P_{t+s}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)\exp(-\lambda z)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-y\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,t+s}_{\sigma}\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-y\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\mathscr{L}^{\,s}_{\sigma}\cdot u_{\lambda}^{D_{t+s}}(x,s)\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-y\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\mathscr{L}^{\,s}_{\sigma}\cdot u_{\lambda}^{D_{t}}(x,0)\big)\Big)\Big)=\exp\Big(-y\cdot u^{D_{s}}_{u_{\lambda}^{D_{t}}(x,0)}\big(x,0\big)\Big),

which coincides with the Laplace transform of the measure
\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{s}(y,{\mathrm{d}}u)P_{t}(u,{\mathrm{d}}z)
. Since
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\leq 1
by (
3.4
) and
1-\exp(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\leq\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}
, we deduce by dominated convergence and (
4.13
) that

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{t}(y,{\mathrm{d}}z)\,z=y\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\leq y.

Since the semigroup clearly fulfils the branching property, it follows that there exits a CSBP associated with the semigroup
(P_{t})
.

Recall the notation
T_{D_{\varepsilon}}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\tau_{\varepsilon}(\overline{W}_{t})<\infty\}
as well as the definition of the sigma field
\mathcal{F}^{D_{\varepsilon}}
from (
3.2
). We will now show that for any
\varepsilon>0
, the process
(\mathscr{L}^{\,\varepsilon+r}_{\sigma}:r\geq 0)
under the probability measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{D_{\varepsilon}}:=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\,\cdot\,|T_{D_{\varepsilon}}<\infty)
has transition kernel
(P_{t})
. Fix
\varepsilon<a<b
; by considering the point process of excursions (
3.21
) outside
D_{a}
, we deduce by an application of the special Markov property that

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\exp\left(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,b}_{\sigma}\right)\big|\mathcal{F}^{D_{a}}\right)=\exp\left(-\mathscr{L}^{\,a}_{\sigma}\mathbb{N}_{x,a}\left(1-\exp(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,b}_{\sigma})\right)\right)=\exp\left(-\mathscr{L}^{\,a}_{\sigma}\cdot u_{\lambda}^{D_{b-a}}(x,0)\right),\quad\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{D_{\varepsilon}}\text{ --a.s.},

where in the last equality we used the translation invariance of the local time of
\xi
. We have obtained that, for every
\varepsilon>0
,
(\mathscr{L}^{\,r+\varepsilon}_{\sigma}:\penalty\ r\geq 0)
under
\mathbb{N}^{D_{\varepsilon}}_{x,0}
is a CSBP with Laplace functional
(u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0):\penalty\ r>0)
and initial distribution
\mathbb{N}^{D_{\varepsilon}}_{x,0}(\mathscr{L}^{\,\varepsilon}_{\sigma}\in{\mathrm{d}}x)
with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}^{D_{\varepsilon+r}}:r\geq 0)
(recall that
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}}
-measurable by Proposition
3
and Lemma
7
).
Now, we claim that for any
0<r_{1}<\dots<r_{k}
and any collection of non-negative measurable functions
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r_{i}}_{\sigma})\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\nu_{r_{1}}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1})f_{1}(z_{1})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{r_{2}-r_{1}}(z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}z_{2})f_{2}(z_{2})\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{r_{k}-r_{k-1}}(z_{k-1},{\mathrm{d}}z_{k})f_{k}(z_{k}).

(4.14)

This follows from the previous result, by observing that for any
\varepsilon<r_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r_{i}}_{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\{T_{D_{\varepsilon}}<\infty\}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{T_{D_{\varepsilon}}<\infty\}}f_{1}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r_{1}}_{\sigma})\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{r_{2}-r_{1}}(\mathscr{L}^{\,r_{1}}_{\sigma},{\mathrm{d}}z_{2})f_{2}(z_{2})\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}P_{r_{k}-r_{k-1}}(z_{k-1},{\mathrm{d}}z_{k})f_{k}(z_{k})\right),

and we conclude taking the limit as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. The fact that the family
(\nu_{t}:t\geq 0)
satisfies that
\nu_{t+s}=\nu_{s}P_{t}
for
t,s\geq 0
now follows from (
4.14
). Let us now identify
\widetilde{\psi}
. Recall from our discussion in (
4.11
) that the Laplace exponent
(u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0):r,\lambda\geq 0)
is the unique solution to the equation

u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)+\int_{0}^{r}{\rm{d}}u\,\Psi\big(u_{\lambda}^{D_{u}}(x,0)\big)=\lambda,

(4.15)

where
\Psi
is the branching mechanism associated with
(P_{t})
, and that it is defined in a unique way by (
4.15
). In particular,
\Psi

characterizes completely the semigroup
(P_{t})
. To identify the branching mechanism we argue as follows: first, observe that the identity (
3.23
) applied at the domain
D_{r}
yields

u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)+\Pi_{x,0}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D_{r}}}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \psi(u^{D_{r}}_{\lambda}(\xi_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t}))\right)=\lambda,

(4.16)

for every
\lambda\geq 0
and
r>0
.
Next, by excursion theory and

(

H
3

)

we get:

\Pi_{x,0}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau_{D_{r}}}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \psi(u^{D_{r}}_{\lambda}(\xi_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t}))\right)=\int_{0}^{r}{\rm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \psi\left(u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(\xi_{t},u)\right)\right)=\int_{0}^{r}{\rm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \psi\left(u_{\lambda}^{D_{r-u}}(\xi_{t},0)\right)\right),

where in the last equality we use the invariance by translation of the local time of
\xi
. Moreover, the special Markov property applied at the domain
D_{0}
gives

u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(y,0)=u_{u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)}(y),

for every
y\in E\setminus\{x\}
and
\lambda\geq 0
– and the identity also holds for
y=x
.
Putting everything together, by definition of
\widetilde{\psi}
, the identity (
4.16
) can be re-written as follows:

u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)+\int_{0}^{r}{\rm{d}}u\,\widetilde{\psi}(u_{\lambda}^{D_{u}}(x,0))=\lambda.

(4.17)

Consequently, we deduce that the branching mechanism associated with the Laplace functional
u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)
is
\widetilde{\psi}
. It remains to show that the conditions stated in Section
2.1
are satisfied by
\widetilde{\psi}
. As we already mentioned, it only remains to verify (A4). In this direction and recalling the notation
T_{D_{r}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}\geq r\}
, also by (
4.17
) we obtain that
f(\lambda,r):=u_{\lambda}^{D_{r}}(x,0)
satisfies for every
r
,

\int_{f(\lambda,r)}^{\lambda}\frac{\rm{d}s}{\widetilde{\psi}(s)}=r,

(4.18)

where the limit
f(\infty,r)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(L_{\sigma}^{D_{r}}>0)
is finite, since
\{L_{\sigma}^{D_{r}}>0\}\subset\{T_{D_{r}}<\infty\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(T_{D_{r}}<\infty)<\infty
by the same argument used before Theorem
3.2
.
Hence, taking the limit as
\lambda\uparrow\infty
in (
4.18
), we infer that the following conditions are fulfilled:

\widetilde{\psi}(\infty)=\infty\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\int^{\infty}_{\cdot}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}s}{\widetilde{\psi}(s)}<\infty.

To derive the exact form of (A4), recall that
\widetilde{\psi}
is convex and that we have
\widetilde{\psi}(0)=0
and
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}(0+)\geq 0
.
∎

Now that we have established that
\widetilde{\psi}
is the Laplace exponent of a Lévy tree, let us briefly introduce some related notation and a few facts that will be used frequently in the upcoming sections. From now on, we set
\widetilde{X}
a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy process and we write
\widetilde{I}
for the running infimum of
\widetilde{X}
. We also denote the excursion measure of the reflected process
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{I}
by
\widetilde{N}
– where the associated local time is
-\widetilde{I}
. The usual notation introduced in Section
2.1
applied to
\widetilde{X}
are indicated with a
\sim
. For instance, we denote the height process and the exploration process issued from
\widetilde{X}
respectively by
\widetilde{H}
and
\widetilde{\rho}
.

By convexity and the fact that
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}(0+)\geq 0
, the only solution to
\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=0
is
\lambda=0
. This implies that the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)
is invertible in
[0,\infty)
. By classical results in the theory of Lévy processes,
\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}
is the Laplace exponent of the right-inverse of
-\widetilde{I}
and, since
\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{I}
does not spend time at
0
, the former is a subordinator with no drift. So, recalling the relation between excursion lengths and jumps of the right-inverse of
-\widetilde{I}
, we derive that:

\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)=\widetilde{N}(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma)),\quad\lambda\geq 0.

(4.19)

For a more detailed discussion, we refer to Chapters IV and VII of
[
4
]
.

We close this section with some useful identities in the same vein of (
4.12
), that will be used frequently in our computations. These identities allow to express some Laplace-like transforms concerning the process
\big(\psi(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{t})):\penalty\ t\geq 0\big)
, under the excursion measure
\mathcal{N}
, in terms of the
\widetilde{\psi}
. As an application of these computations, we will identify the drift and Brownian coefficients of
\widetilde{\psi}
. We summarise these identities in the following lemma.

Lemma 8
.

For every
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
, we have

\mathcal{N}\left(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}\Big)\right)=\frac{\widetilde{\psi}\big(\lambda_{1}\big)-\widetilde{\psi}\big(\lambda_{2}\big)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}.

(4.20)

Recalling the identities (
2.19
), remark that Lemma 8 allows to express the Laplace exponent of
(\widetilde{U}^{(1)},\widetilde{U}^{(2)})
in terms of
\mathcal{N}
and
\psi
.

Proof.

First note that the functions
\lambda\mapsto u_{\lambda}(y)
and
\lambda\mapsto\psi(u_{\lambda}(y))
are non-decreasing. So without loss of generality we can and will assume that
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
. We set
T_{x}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\xi_{t}=x\}
and we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}\cdot\exp\Big(-\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})}\Big)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}\cdot\Pi_{\xi_{s}}\left(\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})}\Big)\right)\right)

where in the last equality we applied the Markov property. On the other hand, the definition of
\widetilde{\psi}
given in (
4.12
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\psi}\big(\lambda_{1}\big)-\widetilde{\psi}\big(\lambda_{2}\big)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}\cdot\frac{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\right).

Consequently, the lemma will follow as soon as we establish the identity:

\frac{u_{\lambda_{1}}(y)-u_{\lambda_{2}}(y)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}=\Pi_{y}\Big(\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})\big)-\psi\big(u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})\big)}{u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{t})-u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{t})}\Big)\Big).

In this direction, recall that under
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}
with
y\neq x
the processes
\mathscr{L}^{\,0}(\rho,\overline{W})
and
L^{E_{*}}(\rho,W)
are well defined and indistinguishables, and remark that

\displaystyle u_{\lambda_{1}}(y)-u_{\lambda_{2}}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{u}\big)-\exp\big(-\lambda_{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{u}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\cdot\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{s}\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{u}\big)\cdot\exp\big(-\lambda_{2}\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{u}\big)\Big).

Then, an application of the Markov property gives:

u_{\lambda_{1}}(y)-u_{\lambda_{2}}(y)=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\cdot\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{s}\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{s}\big)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}\big[\exp\big(-\lambda_{2}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}\big)\big]\Big).

We can now apply the duality identity
\big((\rho_{(\sigma-t)-},\eta_{(\sigma-t)-},\overline{W}_{\sigma-t}):\penalty\ t\in[0,\sigma]\big)\overset{(d)}{=}\big((\eta_{t},\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t}):\penalty\ t\in[0,\sigma]\big)
under
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}
, to get that the previous display is equal to

\displaystyle(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\cdot\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{s}

\displaystyle\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{t}\big)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}\big[\exp\big(-\lambda_{2}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}\big)\big]\Big)

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\cdot\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{s}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}\big[\exp\big(-\lambda_{1}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}\big)\big]\cdot\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}\big[\exp\big(-\lambda_{2}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}\big)\big]\Big).

Remark that
(\eta,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
by duality and right-continuity of
\eta
with respect to the total variation distance.
We are now in position to apply the many-to-one equation (
2.20
). In this direction, for
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
with
\overline{{\text{w}}}(0)=(y,0)
and
y\neq x
we notice that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{{\dagger}}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}\big)\Big]=\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\tau_{D_{0}}(\overline{{\text{w}}})}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma})\big)\Big)=\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\tau_{D_{0}}(\overline{{\text{w}}})}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ u_{\lambda}({\text{w}}(h))\Big),

for every
\lambda>0
. Consequently, (
2.20
) gives:

\displaystyle\frac{u_{\lambda_{1}}(y)-u_{\lambda_{2}}(y)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{y}\Big(\exp\big(-\alpha T_{x}\big)\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{x}}J({\mathrm{d}}s)\penalty\ u_{\lambda_{1}}(\xi_{s})-\int_{0}^{T_{x}}\widecheck{J}({\mathrm{d}}s)\penalty\ u_{\lambda_{2}}(\xi_{s})\Big)\Big).

Finally an application of (
2.19
) yields exactly the desired result (
4.1
).
∎

As an immediate consequence, we obtain two other useful identities taking
\lambda_{2}=0
and letting
\lambda_{2}\downarrow\lambda_{1}
respectively. For every
\lambda>0
, we have

\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \psi\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)/u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\Big)\Big)=\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)/\lambda\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \psi^{\prime}(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h}))\Big)\Big)=\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}(\lambda),

(4.21)

where for the first one we used that
u_{0}(y)=0
since
\mathbb{N}_{y}(L^{E_{*}}_{\sigma}=\infty)=0
. We also stress that (
4.21
) can be proved independently directly by the same arguments as the ones applied in the proof of (
4.20
).

Since by Proposition
5
the exponent
\widetilde{\psi}
satisfies (A1) – (A4), it can be written in the following form

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\alpha}\lambda+\widetilde{\beta}\lambda^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}x)\,(\exp(-\lambda x)-1+\lambda x),

where
\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta}\geq 0
and
\widetilde{\pi}
is a measure in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
\int\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}x)(x\wedge x^{2})<\infty
. In the following corollary, we identify the coefficients
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
.

Corollary 2
.

We have
\widetilde{\alpha}=\mathcal{N}\big(1-\exp(-\alpha\sigma)\big)
and
\widetilde{\beta}=0
.

Proof.

To simplify notation, for
\lambda\geq 0
set
{\psi}^{*}(\lambda):={\psi}(\lambda)/\lambda
,
\widetilde{\psi}^{*}(\lambda):=\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)/\lambda
. Since
\widetilde{\psi}
satisfies (A1)–(A4), by Fubini we derive that
\widetilde{\psi}^{*}
is the Laplace exponent of a subordinator with exponent:

\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}\lambda+\int_{\mathbb{R_{+}}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\widetilde{\pi}([r,\infty))\big(1-\exp(-\lambda r)\big).

(4.22)

Next, introduce the measure
\mathcal{N}^{*}({\mathrm{d}}\xi):=\mathcal{N}(\exp(-\alpha\sigma){\mathrm{d}}\xi)
and observe that by (
4.21
),
\widetilde{\psi}^{*}(\lambda)
can also be written in the form

\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \psi^{*}\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)\Big)\Big)=\mathcal{N}\big(1-\exp(-\alpha\sigma)\big)+\mathcal{N}^{*}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \big(\psi^{*}\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)-\alpha\big)\Big)\Big).

(4.23)

Comparing with (
4.22
), our result will follow by showing that the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.23
) is the Laplace exponent of some pure-jump subordinator. In this direction, introduce under
E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{*}
and conditionally on
(J_{\infty},\xi)
, a Poisson point measure

\mathcal{M}({\mathrm{d}}h,{\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})},

with intensity
J_{\sigma}({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\xi(h),0}\big({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}\big)
. This is always possible up to enlarging the measure space and for simplicity we still denote the underlying measure by
E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{*}
. Next, define the functional
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
and denote its distribution by
\nu({\mathrm{d}}x)
. By definition, we have:

\displaystyle E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{*}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\lambda\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{L}^{\,0}_{\sigma}(\rho^{i},W^{i})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=E^{0}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{*}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}J_{\sigma}({\mathrm{d}}h)u_{\lambda}\big(\xi(h)\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{*}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \big(\psi^{*}\big(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h})\big)-\alpha\big)\Big)\Big),

where in the last equality we used that
J_{\infty}
is the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure of a subordinator with exponent
\psi^{*}(\lambda)-\alpha
. Since the latter expression is finite, we deduce that
\nu
is a Lévy measure satisfying
\int\nu({\mathrm{d}}r)\,(1\wedge r)<\infty
, and that the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.23
) is the Laplace exponent of a driftless subordinator with Lévy measure given by
\nu
.
∎

4.2
Construction of the additive functional
(A_{t})

We are finally in position to introduce our additive function:

Proposition 6
.

Fix
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
and
(\mu,\overline{\emph{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
, the process defined as

A_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{t},\quad\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

is a continuous additive functional of the Lévy snake taking finite values. Furthermore, we have

A_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}},

(4.24)

where the convergence holds uniformly in compact intervals in measure under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}(\,\cdot\,\cap\{\sigma>z\})
for every
z>0
.

Proof.

We start proving the proposition under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
, where
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}):=(\mu,{\text{w}},\ell)\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Remark that by the translation invariance of the local time we might assume that
\ell(0)=0
without loss of generality. For simplicity, we set
y:={\text{w}}(0)
. Next, we write
\widehat{\Lambda}^{*}_{t}:=\sup_{s\leq t}\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}
and we note that it suffices to show that for any
t,K>0

\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\int_{\mathbb{R_{+}}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\,\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{\widehat{\Lambda}^{*}_{t}<K\}}\Big]\rightarrow 0,

as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. In this direction, we remark that the previous expression is bounded above by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\,\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{\widehat{\Lambda}^{*}_{t}<K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq\int_{(0,K]}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\Big],

since on the event
\{\widehat{\Lambda}^{*}_{t}<K\}
we have
\mathscr{L}^{r}=0
for every
r>K
. Now, by Lemma
7
, it suffices to show that the expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
in the previous display is uniformly bounded on
\varepsilon,r>0

since the desired result then follows by dominated convergence. To do so, we set
T_{0}^{+}:=\inf\big\{t\geq 0:\langle\rho_{t},1\rangle=0\big\}
and we notice that by the strong Markov property, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
, the distribution of
(\rho_{T_{0}^{+}+s},\overline{W}_{T_{0}^{+}+s}:\penalty\ s\geq 0)
is
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W})
. In particular we have the upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\Big]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{\dagger}\Big[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}+\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\Big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\Big].

So to conclude we need to prove both:

\displaystyle\text{(i)}\quad\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\sup_{r>0}\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\Big]<\infty;

\displaystyle\text{(ii)}\quad\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\sup_{r>0}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{\dagger}\Big[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}+\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\Big]<\infty.

Let us start showing (i). We are going to apply similar techniques to the ones used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. In this direction, we work under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,0}
and we fix
r,\varepsilon>0
. Now, recall the definition of
\gamma^{D_{r}}
,
\sigma^{D_{r}}
and
\rho^{D_{r}}
introduced in Section
3.2
(keeping in mind the fact that here we work with
(\rho,\overline{W})
) and set

R^{D_{r}}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s\mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma^{D_{r}}_{s}>0\}},\quad\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

which is the right inverse of
\sigma^{D_{r}}
. Next, for every
r>0
, by definition we have
\tau_{r}(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})=\tau_{D_{r}}(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})
and we derive that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R_{s}^{D_{r}}}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{D_{r}}_{u})<\varepsilon\}},

since
on
\{u\geq 0:\,H(\rho_{\sigma^{D_{r}}_{u}})>\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{\sigma^{D_{r}}_{u}})\}
, we have
H(\rho_{u}^{D_{r}})=H(\rho_{\sigma_{u}^{D_{r}}})-\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{\sigma_{u}^{D_{r}}}).

Recall from (
3.19
) that
\langle\rho^{D_{r}},1\rangle
is distributed as
\langle\rho,1\rangle
under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,0}
, which is a reflected
\psi
-Lévy process, and that we denote its local time at
0
by
\ell^{D_{r}}
. In particular, the distribution of
(\langle\rho^{D_{r}},1\rangle,\ell^{D_{r}})
is the same as
\big((X_{t}-I_{t},-I_{t}):\penalty\ t\geq 0\big)
.
Recalling from (
3.20
) that
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{t}=\ell^{D_{r}}(R^{D_{r}}_{t})
and noticing that
R^{D_{r}}_{s}\leq s
, we derive the following inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}|\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{R^{D_{r}}_{s}}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{D_{r}}_{u})<\varepsilon\}}-\ell^{D_{r}}(R^{D_{r}}_{s})|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{D_{r}}_{u})<\varepsilon\}}-\ell^{D_{r}}(s)|\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{0,y,0}\Big[\sup_{s\leq t}|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{u})<\varepsilon\}}+I_{s}|\Big],

where in the first line we used that for each fixed
r>0
, the processes
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
and
L^{D_{r}}
are indistinguishable.
The latter quantity does not depend on
r
and by (
2.5
) it converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, giving (i).

We now turn our attention to the proof of (ii).
On the one hand, by Proposition
2
- (ii) and (
3.4
), for every
r>0
we have

\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\big[\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\big]=\int_{(0,\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}))}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}\big(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\big)=\int_{(0,\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}))}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\xi,\mathcal{L})<\infty\}}\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{r}(\xi,\mathcal{L})\big)\big]\leq\langle\mu,1\rangle.

On the other hand, the remaining term

\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{\dagger}\Big[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}\Big]

can be bounded similarly as we did in (
3.9
). More precisely, consider under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{{\dagger}}
the random measure
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
defined in (
2.18
), set
T:=\inf\{t>0:H_{t}=\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})\}
, with the convention
T=0
if
\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})=\infty
, and remark that for every
s\in[0,T]
we have
\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})=\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})
. Recalling
\mu(\{\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})\})=0
, it follows by considering the excursion intervals of
H
over its running infimum and our previous remark, that the integral
\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}
can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{i}>\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})}\int_{0}^{\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})<h_{i}+H(\rho^{i}_{u})<\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})+\varepsilon\}}+\sum_{h_{i}<\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})}\int_{0}^{\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}^{i}_{u})<H(\rho^{i}_{u})<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u}^{i})\}},

where the first term is now bounded above by

\sum_{h_{i}>\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}})}\int_{0}^{\sigma(\overline{W}^{i})}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho^{i}_{u})<\varepsilon\}}.

Consequently, by (
2.20
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\mu((\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}),\infty))N(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<H(\rho_{s})<\varepsilon\}})+\int_{(0,\,\tau_{r}(\overline{{\text{w}}}))}\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}\big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}s\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})<H_{s}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{s})+\varepsilon\}}\big),

and again by the many-to-one formula (
2.20
), the previous display is bounded by
\varepsilon\cdot\langle\mu,1\rangle
. Putting everything together we deduce the upper bound

\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}+\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\Big]\leq 2\cdot\,\langle\mu,1\rangle,

which does not depend on the pair
r,\varepsilon>0
and concludes the proof of (ii).

Finally, we extend the result under the excursion measure
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
. Working under
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
fix
z>0
and denote by
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})=(\rho_{(g+\cdot)\wedge d},\overline{W}_{(g+\cdot)\wedge d})
the first excursion with length
\sigma>z
. By the previous result, the quantity

\displaystyle\sup_{s\leq t}\Big|\penalty\ \varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}^{\prime}_{u})<H(\rho_{u}^{\prime})<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}^{\prime}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})\penalty\ \Big|

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\sup_{s\leq t\wedge(d-g)}\Big|\penalty\ \varepsilon^{-1}\int_{g}^{g+s}{\mathrm{d}}u\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})<H_{u}<\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{u})+\varepsilon\}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{g+s}-\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{g})\penalty\ \Big|

converges in probability to
0
, and it then follows that (
4.24
) holds in measure under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}(\,\cdot\,\cap\{\sigma>z\})
.

∎

As a straight consequence of the definition of
A
we deduce the following many-to-one formula:

Lemma 9
.

For any non-negative measurable function
\Phi
on
M_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times M_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}}
and
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
, we have

\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\penalty\ \Phi\left(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\right)\right)=\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{y,r_{0}}\left(\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{r}\big)\cdot\Phi\big(J_{\tau_{r}},\widecheck{J}_{\tau_{r}},(\xi_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t}:t\leq\tau_{r})\big)\right).

(4.25)

Proof.

By the translation invariance of the local time it is enough to prove the Lemma for
r_{0}=0
. Now recall that, under
\mathbb{N}_{y,0}
, for every fixed
r\geq 0
the processes
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
and
L^{D_{r}}
are indistinguishable. Consequently, the left-hand side of (
4.25
) can be written in the form:

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}{L}_{s}^{D_{r}}\penalty\ \Phi\left(\rho_{s},\eta_{s},\overline{W}_{s}\right)\right),

and hence we arrive at (
4.25
) applying (
3.4
).
∎

A first consequence of Lemma
9
is that for any
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
, we have

\text{supp}\,{\mathrm{d}}A\subset\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\},\quad\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\text{-- a.e.}

(4.26)

Indeed, it suffices to observe that by (
4.25
), for any
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{\mathrm{d}}_{E}(\widehat{W}_{s},x)>\varepsilon\}}\right)=0,

where we recall that
d_{E}
stands for the metric of
E
.
Let us comment on a few useful identities that will be used frequently in our computations:

Remark
.
Fix
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
with
y\neq x
.
Under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
or
\mathbb{P}_{0,y,r_{0}}
, let
(g,d)
be an interval such that
H_{s}>H_{g}=H_{d}
, for every
s\in(g,d)
, and
\widehat{\Lambda}_{g}=r_{0}
– remark that in particular we have
p_{H}(g)=p_{H}(d)
. We denote the corresponding subtrajectory, in the sense of Section
2.3
, by
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
and its duration by
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma(W^{\prime})
.
Since for any
q\geq r
and
s\geq 0
:

\displaystyle H_{(g+s)\wedge d}=H_{g}+H(\rho^{i}_{s\wedge\sigma_{i}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tau_{q}(\overline{W}_{(g+s)\wedge d})=H_{g}+\tau_{q}(\overline{W}_{s\wedge\sigma_{i}}^{i}),

we deduce by the approximation (
4.24
) that the process
(A_{(g+t)\wedge d}-A_{g}:t\geq 0)
only depends on
(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime})
and it will be denoted by
(A_{s}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{W}^{\prime}):s\geq 0)
. Now we make the following observations:

(i) Working under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
, we denote the connected components of the open set
\{(H_{s}-\tau_{r_{0}}(\overline{W}_{s}))_{+}>0\}
by
((\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):i\in\mathcal{I})
and we set
\sigma_{i}:=\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}
its duration. We also write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
for the excursions from
D_{r_{0}}
corresponding to the interval
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
. By Proposition
6
, the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
does not charge the set

\{s\geq 0:H_{s}\leq\tau_{r_{0}}(\overline{W}_{s})\}
and we derive that:

A_{\sigma}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\int_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}A_{\sigma_{i}}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),\quad\quad\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\text{--a.e.}

(4.27)

(ii) We will now make similar remarks holding under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}^{\dagger}
, for
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
,
denote the connected components of
\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ H_{s}>\inf_{[0,s]}H\}
by
((a_{i},b_{i}):i\in\mathbb{N})
and write
(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})
for the subtrajectory associated with
[a_{i},b_{i}]
. We also set
h_{i}=H_{a_{i}}
and recall that the measure
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
is the Poisson point measure (
2.18
) associated with
(\rho,\overline{W})
. Moreover, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\big[|A_{\sigma}-\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{\sigma}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})|\big]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathrm{d}}r\penalty\ \mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\big[|\mathscr{L}_{\sigma}^{\,r}-\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{L}_{\sigma}^{\,r}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})|\big].

Consequently, by Proposition
2
- (ii), the previous quantity is null and it follows that we still have

A_{\sigma}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A_{\sigma}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i}),\quad\quad\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\text{-- a.s.}

(4.28)

Recall now the definition (
4.12
) of
\widetilde{\psi}
and the notation
u_{\lambda}
introduced in (
4.10
). The following proposition relates the Laplace transform of the total mass
A_{\sigma}
under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and the Laplace exponent
\widetilde{\psi}
. This identity will be needed to characterize the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A
and will also play a central role in Section
5
.

Proposition 7
.

For every
r_{0},\lambda\geq 0
and
y\in E
, we have

\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\Big)=u_{\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)}(y),

where we recall the convention
u_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda
, for every
\lambda\geq 0
. Moreover, for
(\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{\mathrm{w}}}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\Big]=\exp\Big(-\int\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ u_{\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)}(\emph{w}(h))\Big).

The proposition has the following consequence: since
\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)=\widetilde{N}(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma))
, the total mass
A_{\infty}
under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\sigma
under
\widetilde{N}
have the same distribution. This connection is the tip of the iceberg of the results that will be established in the upcoming section, where we establish that the tree structure of the set
\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}:\penalty\ \xi_{\upsilon}=x\}
is encoded by a
\widetilde{\psi}
–Lévy tree.

Proof.

Under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
with
y\neq x
and
r_{0}\geq 0
, set

T^{*}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\tau_{r_{0}}(\overline{W}_{t})<\infty\},

which is just the first hitting time of
x
by
(\widehat{W}_{t})_{t\in[0,\sigma]}
. Notice that by (
4.26
),
A_{\infty}
vanishes on
\{T^{*}=\infty\}

\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
-a.e.. We set
G_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(1-\exp(-\lambda A_{\infty}))
, and remark that the identity (
4.27
) and the special Markov property applied to the domain
D_{r_{0}}
yields:

\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\mathscr{L}^{r_{0}}_{\sigma}\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,r_{0}}\big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\big)\Big)\Big).

Next, by the translation invariance of the local time
\mathcal{L}
, we derive that the previous display is equal to:

\mathbb{N}_{y,0}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\mathscr{L}^{0}_{\sigma}\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\big)\Big)\Big)=u_{G_{\lambda}}(y).

Moreover, for
(\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
if we denote under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
the Poisson process introduced in (
2.18
) by
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
, we get :

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}A_{\infty}(\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\int\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}\big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty}\big)\big)\Big)=\exp\Big(-\int\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ u_{G_{\lambda}}({\text{w}}(h))\Big),

where in the first equality we applied (
4.28
), and in the second we used that
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\delta_{(h_{i},\rho^{i},\overline{W}^{i})}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mu({\mathrm{d}}h)\mathbb{N}_{\overline{{\text{w}}}(h)}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}W)
. Consequently, the statement of the proposition will now follow if we establish that
G_{\lambda}=\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)
. In this direction, for
\lambda>0
, notice that the Markov property implies that

\displaystyle G_{\lambda}=\lambda\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\penalty\ \exp\big(-\lambda\int_{s}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{u}\big)\Big)=\lambda\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\penalty\ \mathbb{E}^{{\dagger}}_{\rho_{s},\overline{W}_{s}}\Big[\exp\big(-\lambda\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{u}\big)\Big]\Big).

By the previous discussion under
\mathbb{P}^{{\dagger}}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
and the many-to-one formula of
A
given in Lemma (
9
), we get:

\displaystyle G_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\penalty\ E^{0}\otimes\Pi_{x,0}\Big(\exp\big(-\alpha\tau_{r}\big)\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\tau_{r}}J_{\tau_{r}}({\mathrm{d}}h)\penalty\ u_{G_{\lambda}}\big(\xi(h)\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\penalty\ \Pi_{x,0}\Big(\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\tau_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi(h))\big)}{u_{G_{\lambda}}\big(\xi(h)\big)}\Big)\Big),

where we recall that
\tau_{r}(\xi,\mathcal{L}):=\inf\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ \mathcal{L}_{s}\geq r\}
and in the second equality we used that
J_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}h)
is the Lebesgue-Stieltjets measure of a subordinator with exponent
\psi(\lambda)/\lambda-\alpha
. Next, under
\Pi_{x,0}
, we consider
(s_{i},t_{i})_{i\geq 1}
the connected components of
\{s\geq 0:\penalty\ \xi_{s}\neq x\}
and we remark that:

\int_{0}^{\tau_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi(h))\big)}{u_{G_{\lambda}}\big(\xi(h)\big)}=\sum\limits_{i\geq 1,\mathcal{L}_{s_{i}}<r}\int_{s_{i}}^{t_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi(h))\big)}{u_{G_{\lambda}}\big(\xi(h)\big)},

since
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}h\mathbbm{1}_{\{\xi_{h}=x\}}=0
by assumption

(

H
3

)

. Consequently, by excursion theory we get:

\Pi_{x,0}\Big(\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\tau_{r}}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi(h))\big)}{u_{G_{\lambda}}\big(\xi(h)\big)}\Big)\Big)=\exp\Big(-r\cdot\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h}))}{u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h})}\big)\Big)\Big),

and hence

\displaystyle G_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\penalty\ \frac{\psi\big(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h}))}{u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h})}\Big)\Big)^{-1}.

However, by the first identity in (
4.21
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\frac{\psi(u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h}))}{u_{G_{\lambda}}(\xi_{h})}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{\psi}(G_{\lambda})}{G_{\lambda}},

and we derive that
\widetilde{\psi}(G_{\lambda})=\lambda
for
\lambda>0
and equivalently
G_{\lambda}=\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda)
. Finally, since
G_{0}=0
the identity also holds for
\lambda=0
.
∎

4.3
Characterization of the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A

The rest of the section is devoted to the characterisation, under
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{{\text{w}}}}
, of the support of the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
. Our characterisation is given in terms of the constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}
, and of a family of special times for the Lévy snake that will be named
exit times from

x
. Before giving a precise statement we will need several preliminary results under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
. First recall that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, for every
r>0
the processes
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
and
L^{D_{r}}
are indistinguishables – and in particular, by Proposition
3
,
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}}
measurable. Fix
r>0
, recall the notation
\tau_{r}(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})=\tau_{D_{r}}(\rho_{t},\overline{W}_{t})
for
t\geq 0
, and denote the connected components of the open set

\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\tau_{r}(\overline{W}_{t})<H_{t}\}
by
\{(a^{r}_{i},b^{r}_{i}):\,i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
. We write
\{(\rho^{i,r},\overline{W}^{i,r}):\,i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
for the corresponding subtrajectories, where as usual
\overline{W}^{i,r}=(W^{i,r},\Lambda^{i,r})
. Next, recall the notation
\Gamma_{s}^{D}:=\inf\big\{t\geq 0:V_{t}^{D}>s\big\}
for
V^{D}
defined by (
3.1
) and we set:

\theta_{u}^{r}:=\inf\big\{s\geq 0\>:\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\Gamma^{D_{r}}_{s}}>u\big\},\quad\text{ for all }u\in[0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}).

Remark that
\text{tr}_{D_{r}}\widehat{({W},{\Lambda})}_{\theta^{r}_{u}}=(x,r)
, for every
u\in[0,\mathscr{L}_{\sigma}^{\,r})
. An application of the special Markov property applied at the domain
D_{r}
gives that, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}}
, the point measure of the excursions from
D_{r}

\mathcal{M}^{(r)}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}}\delta_{(\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{a_{i}^{r}},\rho^{i,r},\overline{W}^{i,r})}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}]}(u){\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\left({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}\right)
.

Lemma 10
.

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
–a.e., we have
\{0,\sigma\}\in\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}A
.

Proof.

We are going to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(A_{\varepsilon\wedge\sigma}=0)=0
– the Lemma will follow since the symmetric statement
\mathbb{N}_{x}(A_{\sigma-\varepsilon}=0)=0
will then hold by the duality identity (
2.16
). As previously we write

G_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda A_{\infty})\big)=\widetilde{\psi}^{-1}(\lambda),

where the second equality holds by Proposition
5
. For every positive rational numbers
r
and
q
, we introduce the stopping time

T_{q}^{r}:=\inf\big\{s\geq 0:\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}>q\big\},

with the convention
T_{q}^{r}=\infty
, if
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}\leq q
. Let us prove that

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\big(A_{T_{q}^{r}}=0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}>0\big)=0.

(4.29)

In this direction, set
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{r}:=\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}({\mathrm{d}}\rho,\,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}|\mathscr{L}_{\sigma}^{r}>0)
and using the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{(r)}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}]}(u){\mathrm{d}}u\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\left({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}\right)
, remark that

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\Big(\exp\big(-\lambda A_{T_{q}^{r}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\Big(\exp\big({-\lambda\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}}A_{\sigma}(\rho^{i,r},\overline{W}^{i,r})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{a_{i}}\leq q\}}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\Big(\exp\Big(-(q\wedge\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\cdot\mathbb{N}_{x}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda A_{\infty})\big)\Big)\Big)=\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\Big(\exp\big(-(q\wedge\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\cdot G_{\lambda}\big)\Big),

and hence:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}^{r}(A_{T_{q}^{r}}=0)+\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\big(\exp\big(-\lambda A_{T_{q}^{r}}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{A_{T_{q}^{r}}>0\}}\big)\leq\mathbb{N}^{r}_{x,0}\left(\exp\big(-(q\wedge\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma})\cdot G_{\lambda}\big)\right).

Now (
4.29
) follows taking the limit as
\lambda\uparrow\infty
, since we are working under
\{\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}>0\}
and by Proposition
5
the function
\widetilde{\psi}
satisfies (A4), which gives that
G_{\lambda}
goes to
\infty
when
\lambda\uparrow\infty
. We stress that (
4.29
) holds for any positive rational numbers
r
and
q
. Now fix
\varepsilon>0
, and notice that by the monotonicity of
A
, we have

\big\{A_{\varepsilon\wedge\sigma}=0\penalty\ ;\penalty\ T_{q}^{r}<\varepsilon\big\}\subset\big\{A_{T_{q}^{r}}=0\penalty\ ;\penalty\ T_{q}^{r}<\varepsilon\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}>0\big\},

where the last set has null
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
measure by (
4.29
). The identity
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(A_{\varepsilon\wedge\sigma}=0)=0
now will follow as soon as we show that,
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
-a.e. , there exists two positive rational numbers
r
and
q
satisfying that
T_{q}^{r}<\varepsilon
. Said otherwise, we need to establish that the origin is an accumulation point of
\{T_{q}^{r}:\penalty\ r,q\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}\}
. Arguing by contradiction, write

\Omega_{0}=\bigcap_{r,q\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\big\{T_{q}^{r}\geq\varepsilon\big\}=\bigcap_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\big\{T_{q}^{r}\geq\varepsilon\penalty\ :\penalty\ \forall q>0\big\}=\bigcap_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\big\{\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\varepsilon}=0\big\}

where in the last equality we used (
4.29
), and suppose that
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\Omega_{0})>0
. To simplify notation, set
C(r):=\inf\{s\geq 0:\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}>r\}
, and remark that the special Markov property, as stated in Theorem
3.2
, applied to the domain
D_{r}
gives
\{\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\varepsilon}=0\}=\{C(r)\geq\varepsilon\}
. We then derive that

0<\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\bigcap\limits_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\{C(r)\geq\varepsilon\}\Big)=\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big(\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}=0,\,\forall s\in[0,\varepsilon\wedge\sigma]\Big).

However, recalling the definition (
2.15
) of the excursion measure
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
this is in contradiction with the fact that for every
s\in(0,\sigma)
,
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
a.e.,
\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}>0
. Indeed, by definition of the Lévy snake under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, for any fixed
s
the process
(W_{s}(t),\Lambda_{s}(t):t\leq\zeta_{s})
has the distribution of a trajectory of the Markov process
(\xi_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t}:t\geq 0)
under
\Pi_{x,0}
killed at
\zeta_{s}
. We then have
\Lambda_{s}(t)>0
, for every
t>0
, since
\mathcal{L}_{t}>0
,
\Pi_{x,0}
a.s., and
\zeta_{s}=H(\rho_{s})
does not vanish on
(0,\sigma)
.
∎

Define:

\mathcal{C}^{*}:=\Big\{t\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ \sup_{(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\cap[0,\sigma]}\widehat{\Lambda}=\inf_{(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\cap[0,\sigma]}\widehat{\Lambda}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{ for some }\varepsilon>0\Big\},

and remark that – the closure of the – connected components of
\mathcal{C}^{*}
are exactly the constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}
. We will show that the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A
is precisely the complement of
\mathcal{C}^{*}
. In this direction, our goal now is to give an equivalent definition of
\mathcal{C}^{*}
in terms of
H
and
W
, and for this purpose we introduce the notion of exit times.

Definition 3
.

(Exit times from
x
)
A non negative number
t
is said to be an exit time from the point
x
for the process
(\rho,W)
if
\widehat{W}_{t}=x
and there exists
s>0
such that

H_{t}<H_{t+u},\quad\text{ for all }\,u\in(0,s).

The collection of exit times from
x
is denoted by
\emph{Exit}(x)
.

Remark 1
.

Note that, for every
t\in\text{Exit}(x)
, the point
p_{H}(t)
corresponds by definition to an interior point of the Lévy tree and in fact, recalling the result of Proposition
4
,
p_{H}(t)
is a point of multiplicity
2
in
\mathcal{T}_{H}
. In particular, for every
t\in\text{Exit}(x)
, there exists a unique
s>t
such that
p_{H}(t)=p_{H}(s)
and satisfying that:

\widehat{W}_{s}=x\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{s-u}>H_{t}=H_{s}\quad\text{ for all }\,u\in(0,v),

for some
v>0
– in this case, we can take
v:=t-s
. By analogy, we write
\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{Exit}(x)
for the collection of times in
[0,\sigma]
satisfying the previous display.
Remark that the correspondence described above between
\text{Exit}(x)
and
\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{Exit}(x)
defines a bijection. We also stress that the inclusion
\text{Exit}(x)\cup\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{Exit}(x)\subset\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\widehat{W}_{t}=x\}
is a priori strict since we are excluding in our definition potential times that will be mapped by
p_{H}
into leaves with label
x
.

Let us now prove the following technical lemma:

Lemma 11
.

For every fixed
r>0
, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, we have:

\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}=\overline{\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:\,i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}}=\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)\cap\big\{s\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{s}=r\big\}},

(4.30)

and the same identity holds if we replace
\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
by
\overline{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
. In particular, the measure
{\mathrm{d}}A
gives no mass to the complement of
\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
(or
\overline{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
).

Proof.

First remark that if
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}=0
, by the special Markov property applied to the domain
D_{r}
, all the sets appearing in (
4.30
) are empty. Hence, it suffices to show (
4.30
) under
\mathbb{N}_{x}^{r}:=\mathbb{N}_{x}(\cdot\,|\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}>0)
. Moreover, notice that by definition we have:

\{a_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}=\mathrm{Exit}(x)\cap\big\{s\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{s}=r\big\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\{b_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}={{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}\cap\big\{s\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{s}=r\big\}.

To deduce (
4.30
), it is then enough to show that:

\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}=\overline{\{a_{i}^{r}:\,i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}},

since the same equality will hold for
\{a_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
replaced by
\{b_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
, using the duality identity (
2.16
) under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
.

So let us prove the previous display, and we start showing the inclusion
\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}\subset\overline{\{a_{i}^{r}:\,i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}}
. In this direction,
consider
s\in\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
. By the special Markov property the set
\{\mathscr{L}_{a_{i}^{r}}^{\,r}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
is dense in
[0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}]
, which gives that for every
\varepsilon
there exists
i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}
such that
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{(s-\varepsilon)+}<\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{a^{r}_{i}}<\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s+\varepsilon}
. This ensures that
a^{r}_{i}\in(s-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon)
by monotonicity of
\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
. Consequently, the set
\text{supp }\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
is included in the closure of
\{a^{r}_{i}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
. Let us now establish the reverse inclusion by showing that for every
j\in\mathcal{I}_{r}
, we have
a_{j}^{r}\in\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
. In order to prove it, set
R_{t}:=\sum_{\mathscr{L}_{a^{r}_{i}}^{r}\leq t}\sigma(\overline{W}^{i,r})
for
t\geq 0
and notice that it is a càdlàg process since
R_{\infty}\leq\sigma<\infty
. Now remark that by definition, for every
k\in\mathcal{I}_{r}
with
a_{k}^{r}<a_{j}^{r}
, we have:

a_{j}^{r}-a_{k}^{r}\leq R_{\mathscr{L}_{a_{j}^{r}}^{r}-}-R_{\mathscr{L}_{a_{k}^{r}}^{r}-}+\theta^{r}_{\mathscr{L}_{a_{j}^{r}}^{r}}-\theta^{r}_{\mathscr{L}_{a_{k}^{r}}^{r}-}.

Since
\theta^{r}
is monotone, it has a countable number of discontinuities and it follows by the special Markov property – using that
\theta^{r}
is
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}}
-measurable – that all the points
\{\mathscr{L}_{a^{r}_{i}}^{\,r}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are continuity points of
\theta^{r}
. Since
R
is càdlàg, this implies that for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
k\in\mathcal{I}_{r}
such that
a_{j}^{r}-\varepsilon<a_{k}^{r}<a_{j}^{r}
. All the values
\{\mathscr{L}_{a_{i}^{r}}^{\,r}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
being distinct we derive that
a_{j}^{r}\in\mathrm{supp}\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}
, as wanted.
As a consequence of (
4.30
), it follows that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}A_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\notin\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s\notin\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}\right)=0,

and we deduce by duality that
{\mathrm{d}}A
gives no mass to the complement of
\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
– the same result holding for
\overline{{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}
.
∎

The next proposition establishes the connection between the constancy intervals of
\widehat{\Lambda}
, the exit times from
x
and the excursion intervals from
D_{r}
. This is the last result needed to characterise the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A
.

Proposition 8
.

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
–a.e., we have:

\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}=\overline{{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}=\overline{\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}\emph{and}i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}}=[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}.

(4.31)

Proof.

The first step consists in showing

\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}\subset\overline{\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}\text{ and }i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}}.

(4.32)

Remark that by Lemma
11
the other inclusion is satisfied and still holds if we replace
\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
by
\overline{{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}
. In this direction, recall that by Lemma
5
the process
(\rho,\overline{W})
takes values in
\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. In particular, we have

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\text{--a.e., }\text{ for all }q\in(0,\sigma),\,\,\overline{\{h<H_{q}:W_{q}(h)=x\}}=\text{supp }\Lambda_{q}({\mathrm{d}}h),

{(*)}

where we recall that
\text{supp }\Lambda_{q}({\mathrm{d}}h)
is precisely the set

\Big\{t\in[0,\zeta_{q}]:\,\Lambda_{q}(t+h)>\Lambda_{q}(t)\text{ for any }0<h<(H_{q}-t)\text{ or }\Lambda_{q}(t)>\Lambda_{q}(t-h)\text{ for any }0<h<t\Big\}.

We let
\Omega_{0}\subset\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{M}_{f}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\times\mathcal{W}_{\overline{E}})
be a measurable subset with
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\Omega_{0}^{c})=0
at which property
(*)
holds for every
(\uprho,\omega)\in\Omega_{0}
and we argue for fixed
(\uprho,\omega)\in\Omega_{0}
. Fix
t\in\text{Exit}(x)
; by definition, for any
\varepsilon>0
we can find
t<q<t+\varepsilon
such that
H_{t}<H_{r}
for every
r\in(t,q]
.
By our choice of
\Omega_{0}
and the snake property, it must hold either that:

(i)

H_{t}
is a time of right-increase for
\Lambda_{q}
(and in particular
\widehat{\Lambda}_{q}>{\Lambda}_{q}(H_{t})=\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}
), or

(ii)

H_{t}
is
not
a time of right-increase for
\Lambda_{q}
, (and hence
{\Lambda}_{q}(H_{t})>{\Lambda}_{q}(H_{t}-s),\,\forall\,0<s<H_{t}
).

If (i) holds, set
s_{k}:=\sup\{s\in[t,q]:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{s}\leq 2^{-k}\lfloor 2^{k}\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}\rfloor+2^{-k}\}
and remark that we have
s_{k}\in\bigcup_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
, as soon as
\widehat{\Lambda}_{s_{k}}<\widehat{\Lambda}_{q}
. However, this is satisfied for
k
large enough. On the other hand, if (ii) holds we must have
\inf_{[t-\varepsilon,t]}H<H_{t}
since
t
can not be a local infimum for
H
(otherwise,
p_{H}(t)
would be a branching point with label
\widehat{W}_{t}=x
, in contradiction with Proposition
4
). Now, the argument of case (i) holds by working with
s_{k}^{\prime}:=\sup\{s\in[0,t]:\penalty\ \widehat{\Lambda}_{s}\leq 2^{-k}\lfloor 2^{k}\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}\rfloor\}
. This implies that
t
belongs to the closure of
\bigcup_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}}\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}
giving (
4.32
). Moreover, by duality the contention (
4.32
) holds replacing
\text{Exit}(x)
by
{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
, proving the first two equalities in (
4.31
). Consequently, to conclude it is enough to show that:

\overline{\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}\text{ and }i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}}\subset[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}\subset\overline{\text{Exit}(x)\cup{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}.

(4.33)

In this direction, notice that for every
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}
, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,r}
, we have
\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}>r
for every
t\in(0,\sigma)
. Now, an application of the special Markov property applied to the domain
D_{r}
gives that:

\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}\subset[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*},\quad\mathbb{N}_{x,0}-\text{a.e.},

for every
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*}
, and the first inclusion
\subset
in (
4.33
) follows. In order to obtain the remaining inclusion, let
t\in[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}
. By definition, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
t-\varepsilon<t_{1}<t_{2}<t+\varepsilon
such that
\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{1}}<\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{2}}
or
\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{1}}>\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{2}}
. If the first holds, then
\sup\{s\in[t-\varepsilon,t_{2}]:\,\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}\leq\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{1}}\}
is an exit time and the other case follows by taking
\inf\{s\in[t_{1},t_{2}]:\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}\leq\widehat{\Lambda}_{t_{2}}\}
. This ensures that
t
is in the closure of
\text{Exit}(x)\cup{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}
concluding our proof.
∎

Now, we are in position to state and prove the main result of the section:

Theorem 4.3
.

Fix
(y,r_{0})\in\overline{E}
and
(\mu,\overline{\emph{w}})\in\overline{\Theta}_{x}
. Under
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\overline{\emph{w}}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{y,r_{0}}
, we have

\emph{supp}{\mathrm{d}}A=\overline{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}=\overline{{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}}=[0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*},

where we recall the convention
[0,\infty]=[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

First remark that by the special Markov property combined with (
4.27
) and (
4.28
), it is enough to prove the theorem under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
. We start by proving the theorem under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
and remark that by Proposition
8
we only have to establish the first equality. Moreover, by Lemma
11
it only remains to show that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
:

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A\supset\overline{\text{Exit}(x)}.

(4.34)

However, by Lemma
10
we know that
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}(\{0,\sigma\}\cap\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A=\emptyset)=0
, and then using that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}}
the measure
\mathcal{M}^{(r)}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{\,r}_{\sigma}]}(\ell){\mathrm{d}}\ell\penalty\ \mathbb{N}_{x,r}\left({\mathrm{d}}\rho,{\mathrm{d}}\overline{W}\right)
, we derive that:

\mathbb{N}_{x,0}-\text{a.e., }\text{ for all }r\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{*},\,\,\{a_{i}^{r},b_{i}^{r}:i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\}\subset\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A.

Consequently, Proposition
8
implies (
4.34
). Finally, let us briefly explain how to obtain the result under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
. In this direction, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, denote the connected components of
\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:X_{s}-I_{s}\neq 0\}
by
\big((\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}):i\in\mathcal{I}\big)
. Excursion theory and our results under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, give that, under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
, we have:

\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A\cap\cup_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})=\overline{\text{Exit}(x)}\cap\cup_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})=\overline{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{Exit}(x)}\penalty\ \cap\cup_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})=\big([0,\sigma]\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}\big)\cap\cup_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}),

\alpha_{i}\in\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A\cap\overline{\text{Exit}}(x)\cap\big([0,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}\big)
and
\beta_{i}\in\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A\cap{\hskip 0.02843pt\scalebox{0.8}{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{ \begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.0,0.0){\hbox{\char 41\relax}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{0.0}}\end{picture} \hskip 0.02843pt }}\hskip 0.56905pt}{}{\mathrm{Exit}(x)}\cap\big([0,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{C}^{*}\big)
for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
. The desired result now follows since the set
\{\alpha_{i}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}\}
and
\{\beta_{i}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are dense in
\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:X_{s}-I_{s}=0\}
.
∎

5
The tree structure of
\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}:\penalty\ \xi_{\upsilon}=x\}

In this section, we work under the framework introduced at the beginning of Section
4
.
Our goal now is to study the structure of the set
\{\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H}:\xi_{\upsilon}=x\}
and to do so, we encode it by
the subordinate tree of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
with respect to the local time
(\mathcal{L}_{\upsilon}:\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H})
. In this direction, we need to briefly recall the notion of subordination of trees defined in
[
22
]
.

Subordination of trees by increasing functions.
Let
(\mathcal{T},d_{\mathcal{T}},\upsilon_{0})
be an
\mathbb{R}
-tree and recall the standard notation
\preceq_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\curlywedge_{\mathcal{T}}
for the ancestor order and the first common ancestor. Next, consider
g:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
a non-negative continuous function. We say that
g
is non-decreasing if for every
u,v\in\mathcal{T}
:

u\preceq_{\mathcal{T}}v\text{ implies that }g(u)\leq g(v).

When the later holds, we can define a pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{T}
by setting

d_{\mathcal{T}}^{g}(u,v):=g(u)+g(v)-2\cdot g(u\curlywedge_{\mathcal{T}}v),\quad\quad(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}.

(5.1)

The pseudo-distance
d_{\mathcal{T}}^{g}
induces the following equivalence relation on
\mathcal{T}
: for
u,v\in\mathcal{T}
we write

u\sim_{\mathcal{T}}^{g}v\iff d_{\mathcal{T}}^{g}(u,v)=0,

and it was shown in
[
22
]
that
\mathcal{T}^{g}:=(\mathcal{T}/\sim_{\mathcal{T}}^{g},d_{\mathcal{T}}^{g},\upsilon_{0})
is a compact pointed
\mathbb{R}
-tree, where we still denoted the equivalency class of the root of
\mathcal{T}^{g}
by
\upsilon_{0}
. The tree
\mathcal{T}^{g}
is called the subordinate tree of
\mathcal{T}
with respect to
g
and we write
p_{g}^{\mathcal{T}}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{g}
for the canonical projection which associates every
u\in\mathcal{T}
with its
\sim_{\mathcal{T}}^{g}
–equivalency class. Observe that any two points
u,v\in\mathcal{T}
are identified if and only if
g
stays constant on
[u,v]_{\mathcal{T}}
and consequently the subordinate tree is obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by identifying in a single point the components of
\mathcal{T}
where
g
is constant.

Getting back to our setting, recall that under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
,
(\mathcal{L}_{\upsilon}:\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H})
corresponds to
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}:t\geq 0)
in the quotient space
\mathcal{T}_{H}=[0,\sigma]/\sim_{H}
. This entails that the local time
(\mathcal{L}_{\upsilon}:\upsilon\in\mathcal{T}_{H})
is a non-decreasing function on
\mathcal{T}_{H}
and we denote the induced subordinate tree by
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
. Recall that the exponent

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=\mathcal{N}\left(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\mathrm{d}}h\,\psi(u_{\lambda}(\xi_{h}))\right),\quad\text{ for }\,\lambda\geq 0,

is the exponent of a Lévy tree by Proposition
5
.
Hence, it satisfies (A1)—(A4) and by Corollary
2
it can be written in the following form:

\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda):=\widetilde{\alpha}\lambda+\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}x)(\exp(-\lambda x)-1+\lambda x),

where
\widetilde{\alpha}=\mathcal{N}\big(1-\exp(-\alpha\sigma)\big)
and
\widetilde{\pi}
is a sigma-finite measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying
\int_{(0,\infty)}\widetilde{\pi}({\mathrm{d}}x)(x\wedge x^{2})<\infty
. We will also use the notation
\widetilde{H}
and
\widetilde{N}
introduced prior to (
4.19
) for the height process and the excursion measure of a
\widetilde{\psi}
–Lévy tree. Finally, we recall that
A
stands for the additive functional introduced in Proposition
6
and we denote its right inverse by
A_{t}^{-1}:=\inf\{s\geq 0:A_{s}>t\}
, with the convention
A^{-1}_{t}=\sigma
for every
t\geq A_{\infty}=A_{\sigma}
. Remark that the constancy intervals of
A
in
[0,\sigma]
are the connected components of
[0,\sigma]\setminus\text{supp }{\mathrm{d}}A
, which by Theorem
4.3
are precisely the connected components of
\mathcal{C}^{*}
– the constancy intervals of the process
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in[0,\sigma])
. In particular,
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{t}}:t\geq 0)
is a continuous non-negative process, with lifetime
A_{\infty}
. We can now state the main result of this section:

Theorem 5.1
.

The following properties hold:

(i)

Under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the subordinate tree of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
with respect to the local time
\mathcal{L}
, that we denote by
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
, is isometric to the tree coded by the continuous function
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{t}}:\penalty\ t\geq 0).

(ii)

Moreover, we have the equality in distribution

\Big((\widetilde{H}_{t}:\,t\geq 0),\text{ under }\widetilde{N}\Big)\overset{(d)}{=}\Big(\big(\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{t}}:\,t\geq 0\big),\text{ under }\mathbb{N}_{x,0}\Big).

(5.2)

In particular,
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a Lévy tree with exponent
\widetilde{\psi}
.

Remark 2
.

Let us mention that when
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}/2
and the underlying spatial motion
\xi
is a Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}
, the previous theorem implies that under
\mathbb{N}_{0,0}
the subordinate tree of
\mathcal{T}_{H}
with respect to the local time
\mathcal{L}
at
0
is a Lévy tree and – as a direct consequence of the scaling invariance of the Brownian motion – its exponent is of the form
\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=c\lambda^{3/2}
, for some constant
c>0
. This result was already obtained by other methods in
[
22
, Theorem 2]
.

We stress that the key result in (ii) is the identity in distribution (
5.2
): it entails that not only the function
(\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{t}}:t\geq 0)
encodes the subordinate tree, but it is also the height process of a Lévy tree. The fact that
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a
\widetilde{\psi}
-Lévy tree is then a direct consequence of (i) and (
5.2
). By a straightforward application of excursion theory one can deduce a version under
\mathbb{P}_{0,x,0}
of Theorem
5.1
, where now
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a Lévy forest with exponent
\widetilde{\psi}
. The details are left to the reader.

The rest of the section is organised as follows:
The section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.1
. In Section
5.1
we start by showing (i) and we present the strategy that we follow to prove (ii). The proof of (ii) relies in all the machinery developed in previous sections combined with standard properties of Poisson point measures and is the content of Section
5.2
.

5.1
The height process of the subordinate tree

In this short section we establish the first claim of Theorem
5.1
and settle the ground for the second part of the result. For every
u\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, recall that
\mathcal{L}_{u}:=\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}
where
s
is any element of
p_{H}^{-1}(\{u\})
(note that the definition is non ambiguous by the snake property) and that
\mathcal{L}
is non-decreasing on
\mathcal{T}_{H}
. To simplify notation, we set:

{H}^{A}_{t}:=\widehat{\Lambda}_{A^{-1}_{t}},\quad t\geq 0,

which is a continuous process – as it was already mentioned in the discussion before Theorem
5.1
. Let us start with the proof of Theorem
5.1
-(i).

Proof of Theorem
5.1
-(i).

Our goal is to show that, under
\mathbb{N}_{x,0}
, the trees
\mathcal{T}_{H^{A}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
are isometric. In this direction, we start by introducing the pseudo-distance:

\widetilde{d}(s,t):=\widehat{\Lambda}_{t}+\widehat{\Lambda}_{s}-2\cdot\min\limits_{s\wedge t,s\vee t}\widehat{\Lambda}\penalty\ ,\quad s,t\in[0,\sigma],

and we write
s\approx t
if and only if
\widetilde{d}(s,t)=0
. By the snake property, we have
s\approx t
for every
s\sim_{H}t
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{L}
is increasing on
\mathcal{T}_{H}
, we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{p_{H}(t)}+\mathcal{L}_{p_{H}(s)}-2\cdot\mathcal{L}_{p_{H}(s)\curlywedge_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}p_{H}(t)},

for every
s,t\in[0,\sigma]
.
The right-hand side of the previous display is exactly the definition of the pseudo-distance associated with the subordinate tree
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
between
p_{H}(s)
and
p_{H}(t)
given in (
5.1
). We deduce that
([0,\sigma]/\approx,\widetilde{d},0)
is isometric to
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{L}}_{H}
. It remains to show that
([0,\sigma]/\approx,\widetilde{d},0)
is also isometric to
(\mathcal{T}_{H^{A}},d_{H^{A}},0)
. In order to prove it, we notice that:

\widetilde{d}(A_{r_{1}}^{-1},A_{r_{2}}^{-1})=d_{H^{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}),

for every
r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,A_{\sigma}]
. Furthermore, for every
t\in[0,\sigma]
there exists
r\in[0,A_{\sigma}]
such that
A_{r-}^{-1}\leq t\leq A_{r}^{-1}
since by Lemma
10
the points
0
and
\sigma
are in the support of
{\mathrm{d}}A
. Moreover we have
\widetilde{d}(A_{r}^{-1},t)=0
, since by Theorem
4.3
the process
\widehat{\Lambda}
stays constant on every interval of the form
[A_{r-}^{-1},A_{r}^{-1}]
. This implies that
[0,\sigma]/\approx\penalty\ =\{A_{r}^{-1}:\penalty\ r\in[0,A_{\infty}]\}/\approx
and we deduce by the previous display that
([0,\sigma]/\approx,\widetilde{d},0)
and
(\mathcal{T}_{H^{A}},d_{H^{A}},0)
are isometric giving the desired result.
∎

The main difficulty to establish Theorem
5.1
(ii) comes from the fact that
\widetilde{H}
is not a Markov process. To circumvent this, we are going to use the notion of marked trees embedded in a function.

Marked trees embedded in a function.

A marked tree is a pair
\textbf{T}:=(\text{T},\{h_{v}:\penalty\ v\in\text{T}\})
, where
T
is a finite rooted ordered tree and
h_{v}\geq 0
for every
v\in\text{T}
– the number
h_{v}
is called the label of the individual
v
. For completeness let us give the formal definition of a rooted ordered tree. First, introduce Ulam’s tree:

\mathcal{U}:=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\{1,2,...\}^{n}

where by convention
\{1,2,...\}^{0}={\varnothing}
. If
u=(u_{1},...u_{m})
and
v=(v_{1},...,v_{n})
belong to
\mathcal{U}
, we write
uv
for the concatenation of
u
and
v
, viz.
(u_{1},...u_{m},v_{1},...,v_{n})
. In particular, we have
u\varnothing=\varnothing u=u
.
A (finite) rooted ordered tree
T
is a finite subset of
\mathcal{U}
such that:

(i)

\varnothing\in\text{T}
;

(ii)

If
v\in\text{T}
and
v=uj
for some
u\in\mathcal{U}
and
j\in\{1,2,...\}
, then
u\in\text{T}
;

(iii)

For every
u\in\text{T}
, there exists a number
k_{u}(\text{T})\geq 0
such that
uj\in\text{T}
if and only if
1\leq j\leq k_{u}(\text{T})
.

If
u\in\text{T}
can be written as
u=vj
for some
v\in\text{T}
,
1\leq j\leq k_{v}(\text{T})
, we say that
v
is the parent of
u
. More generally, if
u=vy
for some
v\in\text{T}
and
y\in\mathcal{U}
with
y\neq\emptyset
, we say that
v
is an ancestor of
u
or equivalently that
u
is a descendant of
v
. On the other hand, if
u\in\text{T}
satisfies that
k_{u}(\text{T})=0
,
u
is called a leaf. The element
\varnothing
